HIS DREAM REALISED.-It was the first time that the Chicago bride had ever been separated from her husband. Her heart, naturally given to inrebodings, beat strong in her breast as the tele- eaph boy slowly approached with stolid indiffer- ence. She took the envelope with a trembling hand and was soon secluded within the privacy of her lonely room. "Can it be that he is sick, or eqn he be dead?" she murmured, with a tearful tremor in her choking voice. One mighty effort and the envelope is broken, while through her blinding tears she reads: "Pork gone skywards. We can now move to Gotham. Hubby."

Mr. Burns breaking completely away from it, that the one abused the other in most unmeasured terms on every possible oppertunity,and that no approach to reconcilement has ever been attempted. But it Mr. Burns has broken away from the Socialists, h ehas not broken away from Socialism. His address as a candidate for a seat on he London County Council has come into my hands, and the most extreme member of the Social Democratic Federation could not draw up a more thoroughgoing code of Social- istic doctrine and aims. He would municipalise the gas, the water,the markets,the docks,the steamboat services on the river,the omnibus services,and elec- tric lighting,and to this list he would probably add others in time. Against Lord Salisbury's idea of reducing the Council to a drainage board, he puts forward a scheme for greatly enlarging its powers, and importance,under which he would make it the organ of complete local government, having the powers of the Scnool Board,the Asylums Board,and the Thames Conservancy Board transferred to it.

The principal electric lighting companies who carry on their operations inLondon are issuing very Ratisfactory reports, and declaring dividends that will prove gratifying to their shareholders. One company,which paid 7 per cent a year ago,has now declared 10,and gives an extremely encouraging ac- count of itself. Another !as earned 7 per cent, but only declared a dividend of 4, as it sets aside the remainder to a depreciation fund. Another declared 4 per cent last year, and now declares 6 per cent, and looks for larger dividends next year.

THE LADY AND TTIE DONKEY CART.

A neaten figure in the little Hertfordshire village of Redbourne, is Mrs. Ann Markham, who drives about in her little donkey cart, as comfortable and happy-looking an old lady as you could wish to see. She resides in her well-appointed house, North Vac., Redbourne, and told a pleasant life story to the repre- sentative of the " Herta Advertiser." "In the first place, she said, "I must tell you my a —sixty-seven years. I suffered from sciatica for twelve months, and could n t get down to the door witheut stick. Doctors told me I should have to lie in bed con- tinually. I could not put my boots on, and suffered very great pain: if I had to do any work I had to put my leg on a stool. Sometimes my leg would give way and let me down,and I had a terrible job to get up and down. stairs.As I bad been a healthy woman I felt this confine- ment to the house very much, though my husband bought me a donkey and cart so that I could get out a little. One day I received through the pest a book telling of the remarkable cures effected by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People,and read it:through.l resolved to try the pills, and did so, with the result that after taking 'four boxes I was able to walk about once more free from pain. Now I can walk a couple of miles, for I went to the school treat yesterday and felt no worse for the exertion. I feel confident it was the pills that cured me, and I've taken no other medicine since I took them." At this moment, the neat little donkey and convey- ance came in sight. "Of course," said Mrs. Markham, "I shall keen the donkey and cart, and enjoy occasional should now lam ne use well, but all li ow Ishad2,not seen that book I The book which Mrs. Markham received is one of the many ways in which the usefulness of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills is made known to the public; but the news of their great cures is constantly being reported in the papers. They are famous among the highest and the lowest in the land for the cure of, anemia, palpitation of the heart, indigestion, genera' muscular weakness, rheumatism, sciatica, chronic ery- sipelas, and to restore pale and galley complexions to thf' glow of heal!h. They are also a splendid nerve and spinal tonic,and thus have cured many cases of paraly- sis, loeornotor ataxy, neuralgia, St. Vitus' dance, and nervous headache. They are now obtainable of all chemists, and from Dr. Williams' Medicine Cornpany,4B, Holborn-viaduct,London,at 2e 9 a box,or six for 13a 9d; but are genuine only with full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. Pink pills sold loose or from glass d jars cannot be Dr. Williams'. and won't cure you : sub- stitutes never cured anyone !

Lord and Lady Battersea have placed on the lintels on their doors and along their railings, just opposite the Marble-arch, boards containing the broadsheets of the Progressive candidates for the district. Electors are invited to vote for Sir Alger- non West and for the Hon. John Wallop; and the humor of the passing 'bus-drivers very naturally exhausts itselt on the name of the sEcond of these candidates. This,when printed on the voting papers as " Wallop,the Hon Jotan," assumes an order which rejoices the enemy, to whom a little guileless pleasure need not be grudged.

It is said that the legal expenses in the Gros- venor lictel case ran to about £OOO a day. As the hearing occupied nine days that would give a sum of £5,400. Having regard to the expenses cif the case before it came into court the whole outlay can hardly have been less tham £lO,OOO.

Madame Patti is to be added to the list of those who have had che uncanny experience of reading their own obituary notices. The papers by Mon- day's Australian mail contain full accounts of her life and professional career. The cable message announcing the death of her husband, M. Nicolini, was misread.

Although the feeling is spreading amongst the Chartered Company's shareholders in favour of put- ting Messrs. Rhodes and Beit on the boards I learn ou good authority that he latter is opposed to his name being brought forward. He does not consider it prudent, either in the interests of the Chartered Company itself or of the gold companies in the Transvaal. It would undoubtedly create alarm and agitation among the Boers, and is at present alto- gether too strong a draught to give President Kruger to swallow. Therefore that part of the pro- posal will probably be dropped, and if there were any disinclination to do so a plain hint from Mr. Chamberlain would pat an end to the idea for the present. To Nigeria and the Nile we need not add a retie v. al of a third trouble in the Transvaal.

The comparatively humble abode of the late Lord Randolph Churchill is placarded with the an- nouncement that it is for sale. "This fine medium sized resklence," as it is discribed, is a curiosity in its way. The fact that a house of dimensions so bumble—it has only one widish window to its en- tire breadth on each of its four storeys—should have been the residence for years of a man who was England's largest financier—as Chancellor of the Exchequer—gives eloquent testimony to the purity of English politicians whose knowledge, used un- scrupulously, or for selfish ends, could make them the easy masters of big fortunes.

The recent death of Sir Henry Havelock-Allan relieved the pension list of a charge of .£l,OOO per annum, which the gallant officer had enjoyed in consideration of the services of his father in the Indian Mutiny ; and now the death of Lord Com- bermere involves the disappearance of another an- puity of ..£2,000 a year conferred by Parliament on the first Viscount and his two successors in the title in respect of his services in the Peninsular War. When in 1885 Parliament decided to reward Lord Wolseley and the late Lord Alcester with lump i sums instead of annual allowances, it practically pronounced against the granting of any further hereditary pensions for naval and military services; but the following, granted in past times, are still payable—viz.,.£72o (.£1,460 of the original allowance having been commut4) to the heirs of the Duke of Scbomberg in perpetuity • ..£2,000 to Lord Rodney and his successors; £5,000 to Earl Nelson and to all u e ; to whom the title shall descend: X 4,000 to the Duke of Wellington,terminable with the life of the present Dk DP® to Viscount Hardinge ; and .£2,000 each te Lord Seaton, Lord Keane, Viscount Gough, Lord Raglan, and Lord Napier of Magdala, all ter- minable with the lives of the existing annuitants.

Opinion among the Ministerialists has crystalisad into the conviction that nothing more will be done °wards a settlement of the Roman Catholic Uni- versity clucmtion during the lifetime of the present Parliament. This amounts to a determination on the aart of the Unionists to bequeath it to their succes- Yore. But the Liberals will no more touch it than the Unionists. They would be less able to carry it than the present Government, with its powerful majority. If the 'Unionists came back to power once more, with a sufficiently strong majority, the gradual transformation of opinion in their ranks would allow of their carrying it ; otherwise, bow- ever, the Nationalist members will have to trust to their securing Home Rule first, and then setting up a university for themselves.

A new, or to speak more strictly a novel, honour was conferred upon Mr. Thomas Bowles in the course of debate on Monday night in the House of Commons. Hitherto the representative of King's Lynn ba-, been alternately known as "The Cap'n." Thlsev.- runw, Mr. Wt-tugh scored a literary success by referrin,.: to the bun. gentleman as "A member Enet, Lynn."

The moment is disagreeably opportune for the appearance cf the Array estimates. They provide for a total increase in men of nearly 22,000 and in expenditure of £1.223,000. While provision is made for augmentinyz the Regular Army to this con.: sidersble extent, Volunteers will observe with some dissatisfaction that the vote asked on behalf of their branch of the service is reduced by £l3 000. The explanation given is that the number of effici- ents is smaller than last year. At a time, however, when the expedincy of conscription is being gravely discussed in responsible quarters,this reduc- tion in the grant to the most economical branch of our military system will inevitably provoke criti clam and complaint.

THE WHEEL OF FATE.

Br MRS. BAGOT Author of "Love's Revenge," '"Bianea," " The Silver Lining," Sco., Sce.

CHAPTER VIII.

Nearly a fortnight had passed uneventfully at Les Avants, judging, at least, by et Irnal appearances. Mrs. Hughes had exercised a vigilant espionage over the Trehernes, even to the extent of taking up her position, knitting in hand, close to Gwendoline whenever she sketched near the hotel. Constance was surprised and amused to notice how patiently her sister tolerated the clicking of those knitting-needles. With a woman's quickness for unravelling love affairs, Mrs. Hughes had discovered that her nephew was dangerously fascinated by Gwendoline's attractions. In her opinion this was a very alarming condition of things—one that demanded diplomatic treatment. She had remonstrated in cautious terms with Dennis on many occasions, but always with the same want of success that had characterised her first interview on the subject. At last she realised that expostulations were vain; so she forthwith decided to seize every possible opportunity for exercising a vigorous chaperonage, so far as Gwendoline was concerned.

Under these circumstances Mrs. Hughes's normally cheerful countenance was gradually assuming a careworn expression; especially as she noticed that the rapprochement between Gwendolino and Dennis was daily growing stronger, notwith- standing her continuous efforts to check it. "Did I not tell you, William, that you had done foolishly by accepting the guardianship of those two girls, and that it would destroy our peace here? " she said one afternoon, when the Trehernes had gone for a walk with Dennis and Eric, with- out leaving any trace behind them of the direction they had taken. "Yes, my dear; you certainly did make aa observation of that description," mildly replied hes husband. "But I do not see how I could have acted differently in the matter; and, all things considered, I do not think that emy harm will arise from it. I like both the Trehernes ; and I fail to see the least impropriety in their behaviour. It is only natural that they should be friendly with Dennis and Hume, as they have known them for some time. As for their having gone for a walk together this afternoon, I should be very sorry to believe they were unworthy of being trusted to do so."

"Of course they can be trusted!" interposed Mrs. Hughes decidedly. "But I am so very much afraid ,that one day, when I am not present, Dennis will propose to Miss Treherne." "If he wishes to propose to her, nothing that you or I can do will prevent him." "But with his risinr, prospects at the Bar he could do so much better. if could marry Lucy Floxham, a sweet, domesticated character—a girl I could love."

Mr. Hughes smiled. He was accustomed to hear his wife dwell upon Lucy Floxham's perfections. Then, with a view to smoothing matters, he said quietly: .... _

"I 'am quite sure, my dear, that you will love the woman Dennis chooses for his wife, provided she is respectable and a lady." "I cannot quite promise that; but the boy is as dear to me as if he were my own son. Poor Phyllis! I shall never forget her asking me, as she was dying, to look after him. Eighteen years of age was young for the lad to be left an orphan and thrown on his own resources. That is why I am so anxious he should marry a sensible person. Have you noticed how frivolously he laughs when Miss Treherne ie near? In her society he seems to give himself up entirely to enjoyment. Did you not observe their behaviour at the picnic yesterday? " "I only noticed that they seemed very happy together. You seem to forget, Maria, that God Almighty's lambs must play. A time comes—only too soon—when merriment is comparatively diffi- cult."

"A little cheerfulness is a good thing, I daresaY," was the doubtfully spoken rejoinder. "However, Mr. Treherne will soon be here now, and he will relieve us from all responsibties. I must say I can't understand your reason for object- ing to his daughters so much." Mrs. Hughes drew herself up indignantly, preparatory to unburdening her mind further on the subject, but a tap at the door came as an opportune diversion. "For Dennis!" she exclaimed, taking a telegram from the garcon,. "I wonder who it is from," she added, with growing alarm, and examining the envelope in a manner that clearly indicated she was most anxious to know what news it contained. "I hope nothing is amiss at home," rejoined her husband thoughtfully. "What do you think we had better do? Shall we open it? How tiresome it is that Dennis is out!" _

"We must certainly not open it, my dear." "Then we'll go and wait—oh I but here they all come, I think. Those must be the Trehernes, in their white flannel dresses." "Yes, those are the Trehernes," said Mr. Hughes, looking through his orra-glasses, "and Dennis and Hume are with them. "We'll go and meet them, William. I'm so very anxious to hear what this telegram is about." Baying this she hastily donned her hat, and left the hotel a moment after, closely followed by her husband _ . .

Dennis received the telegram with a complacent smile, and put it in his pocket unopened. "Oh, my dear boy! surely you are going to open it! " expostulated his aunt in surprise. "Your uncle and I hurried out to meet you, so that you should have it without a moment's loss of time." "Thanks, awfully. But I'm so accustomed to getting telegrams that I don't think any more of receiving one than a letter," he answered, smiling. "But you always hear of sudden deaths and those kind of things by telegram. Do open it at once, dear," expostulated Mrs. Hughes. "Very well, aunt, I'll relieve your anxiety," he answered, dropping behind the others and opening it in a leisui ely manner. His eyes had scarcely fallen on the first words when he pulled himself up to a sudden standstill, and concentrated his atten- tion on the contents. Eric glanced back, and a shade of surprise crossed his face. Mrs. Hughes also glanced round. Her curiosity to know the contents of that tele- gram was greatly augmented by seeing her nephew thrust it in his pocket, with a gesture of annoyance. "William, go back and ask Dennis what news that telegram contains? " she said in a low voice to her husband.

"No. I tla it would be better not to do so, was the reply. Eric knew instinctively that his friend had received unwelcome news, and he at once decided that the kindest thing to do under the circum- stances was to set the example of walking on at a brisk pace, thus leaving him time to digest the news quietly. Mrs. Hughes's conduct on the occasion forcibly reminded him of Lot's wife. She seemed wholly incapable of keeping from looking round every minute. Her husband, however, kept steadily 0/2 his way, and in a short time th6y all entered the hotel, save Dennis. It was fully an hour later when he walked slowly through the hall, looking pale and haggard. Constance happened to come out of the reading- room at that moment, and he turned abruptly towards her and said:

"I am sorry I must say good-bye now. I have just received news that compels me to return at once to London." "I am very sorry that your holiday has been cut short," she answered feelingly. "Thanks—so am I." He spoke in a hard, bitter voice.

"Perhaps you may be able to return here." "Perhaps so. But the chances of my being able to do so are very small." He looked at her in a nervous, hesitating manner; and it was difficult to know what answer to make. After a short pause he added: "I —I should like to say good-bye to your sister." "You will find her sketching near the chalet on the road to Montreux," was the reply. "Thank you." With these words be left her. Constance watched him walking quickly down the road with feelings of curiosity and surprise. Once at the chalet he quitted the high road and entered a path through the woods, which led to one of Gwendoline's favourite nooks-for sketching. He strode on with long, decided strides, and his face bore the expression of a man to whom a cruel task has fallen. Gwendoline saw him approaching, and looked up with a smile. "Have you come to sharpen my pencils again she asked.

lie shooa his head. "I have Come on a very difflrent mission, unfortunately." Then she noticed that he' looked pale and down- cast.

"What has happened? " she asked, a fear of coming sorrow darting across her mind. "I have been suddenly recalled to England. At least, something has occurred that makes it neces- sary for me to return home. I have come to say good-bye to you; lam heartily sorry to leave." He spoke with forced composure. "I am very sorry too; it will spoil your holi- days," she answered sympathetically. "I only hope it will not ruin my life," he said impetuously. "I am afraid you are inclined to take a very dismal view of matters. Surely nothing so very terrible has happened? " "A matter has cropped up again that I hoped was forgotten and buried; and it is probable that I shall now have to face the consequences of a hasty, mad act of my youth. Just after I came of age I was drawn into taking a step that I have regretted ever since. But I don't, know what right I have to bore you with my troubles." "You have every right," she murmured in a low voice, as the colour rose to her cheeks. She realised that this was one of the moments of her life fraught with far-stretching consequences. A moment to be remembered—perhaps regretted— in the long years to come. "Cannot you tell me about the trouble? " she asked, looking up sympathetically into his grave, sad face.

"Are you sure you would like to hear? " she answered in a low voice. "Yes." "Perhaps, when you know all, you will say that I had no right to tell you." "Then I shall blame myself—not you." It was bravely said.

"Are you alWays willing to face the consequences of your acts ? " "Yes; nothing can be gained by shirking them, probably a great deal lost. Only cowards shirk the consequences of their actions." "Then in your eyes I must for ever remain a coward." He spoke almost fiercely. "For instead of fac;ng my position and overcoming difficulties that arose in consequence of that foolish action, which I mentioned just now, I have allowed matters to drift on. I could have settled every- thing two years ago by paying somewhat heavily for my folly. But I hated having to discuss terms with a man that I despised; and who gloried in the power he had gained over me, never missing an opportunity for referring to the past, in a way that maddened me."

------ - "How had he gained such power over you? " she asked. _

"He had two daughters. They were both pretty, and I was mad enough to imagine I-- He broke off abruptly, then added, in a low voice: "I became engaged to the younger one." "When was the engagement broken off? "It has never been really broken off." There was no answer. Gwendoline pressed her lips together. She was very pale now. "You will despise me for concealing all this from my—my friends, he said. "It would have been more honourable to let it be known." She spoke slowly and with an effort. "Yes. I see now that it would. I regret my silence. Some months ago I made every effort to find out where the man, Captain Tewser, was hying, but could not discover any trace of his whereabouts. It was useless my attempting to treat with the daughter, even if I could have traced her. It is not in her nature to take upon herself any respon- sibilities. During the time that I was trying to trace the Tewsers I discovered that the ring which I gave Grace Tewser had been sold to the jeweller from whom I bad bought it. I was also told that she had gone abroad with a sister of hers, who had married a wealthy American. Under these circum- stances I considered myself morally free; especially as we had had no intercourse for two years. To-day Captain Tewser has telegraphed to me, saying my presence is urgently required owing to severe ill- ness.' Now that you have heard all, of course you think I have acted badly." Though he spoke in a voice of conviction, it was evident he hoped she would contradict him.

"You have been a victim to circumstances," she answered hoarsely. "And my own folly," he added bitterly. "Young men are often foolish," she said, trying to smile. "You can undo the past only by acting with scrupulous honour in the future." "Yes. I'm not the only fellow who has wrecked his life," he answered.

"Why do you speak of it aa wrecked? Can you not— She checked herself, realising instinctively that it was not seemly she should advise him in such a matter. "You must not forget that a promise is morally binding," she added; "not a thing to be lightly shirked." Neither spoke for a few minutes; then Dennis extended his hand and said in a calm, resolute, yet broken-hearted voice "Good-bye." "Good-bye," she echoed, taking his band. Com- manding herself with a fierce effort, she added: "In whatever way matters may end, I trust all will be for your happiness." "Thank you." He uttered the words with difficulty, then turned and left her.

As she watched him gradually disappear from her view, sorrow was born in her heart. That most terrible of all sorrows—the sorrow which must be screened from others.

CHAPTER IX. "Grace! Grace!" Captain Tewser spoke in a weak, querulous voice. "I'm coming, father," answered a tall, slight, fair girl, entering hastily from the adjoining room. "Why don't you always come at once ? " "I came as quickly as I could;" she answered gently. "What can I give you? " "Something to drink—quickly." She poured out some milk hurriedly, and then, tenderly raising his head, held it to his lips. "Thanks," he murmured. "Let me raise your pillow," she said, drawing it carefully on one side. "That is better," he said in a voice of relief. "I shall not be here to trouble you much longer, Grace. I've been a long time dying. It hasn't been my fault that it has taken so long. It's been--" Here a severe attack of coughing brought the sentence to an abrupt termination. Immediately Grace raised him in her aims. "Oh, father, I am so grieved! " she exclaimed in a voice full of sympathy. "Don't, pray, imagine for an instant that I mind the trouble of nursing you. It pains me so much to hear you talk like that. Have I ever complained? " "No—no. But it's awful to suffer like_thy,". he

gasped; then he added, almost to himself: "It's a c•miserable ending to a miserable life. I'll do my ' duty to you, Grace, before I die. I will—l will! Ethel has a rich husband and a beautiful home. You shall not be left homeless and poor. Vane isn't a rich man, but he'll stand between you and poverty—thank God l " Suddenly the girl's cheeks became scarlet. "You sent off that telegram to him? " he asked sharey. es.” "And that letter warning me that he was flirting with that Miss Treherne—where is it ?" _ _

"I have destroyed it, as you told me to do." "That's right. He'll be certain to come. He always came quickly in response to my telegrams." "But, father, do you really feel quite well enough to see him, and to be troubled with—with talking ? " she asked gently. "Don't you think it would be best to let matters rest as they are? " "No I " he said emphatically. She knelt down by his side and took his thin, wan hand in hers gently and caressingly. Neither spoke for a few minutes; then Grace broke the silence, speaking with greater decision than her father had ever heard her speak before. "Dennis has long ago ceased to care for me," she said. "I have known it for a long time. Do not take it to heart, father. I can work and support myself. I cannot marry him now that he does not care for me. It would be so wrong, and—" "Don't worry me, child, by talking nonsense ! " he said, interrupting her and turning away. "You know I hate sentiment. Go back to your room and rest. I shan't disturb you again. It won't do for you to look white and jaded when he arrives. It will only mako matters more difficult to arrange." "I don't feel tired now, father. I would rather stay with you." "No—no! Do as I tell you." She rose reluctantly, and turned to leave him. _ . _

, "I want to be left alone," he said, peevishly and in a weak voice; for the effort to speak was gradu- ally growing greater. At the door Grace looked round and glanced furtively at him. Never until this minute had she realised how entirely he, her father, had constituted her world; except for that brief, joyful period when Dennis heAt been an inmate of their home.

The small, ill-kept room did not strike her as un- comfortable. She had grown accustomed to tM cobwebs in the corners and the grimy finger-markB on the walls. A short blind, torn and dirty, was fastened across the window. A solitary flower-pot stood on the window-sill, but the plant had withered and died for lack of watering. Of furniture there was little, and that little was badly placed and neglected. Amidst her sordid surroundings the girl's aristo- cratic beauty stood out in striking relief; and there was a pathetic sweetness in her fair face. But strength to bravely assume responsibilities and to act firmly had no part in her character; of this she was painfully aware. There had been moments when in dire distress of mind she had made an effort to assert her own opinion; but it had invari- ably ended by her giving in an succumbing still more to the will of others.

She loved Dennis Vane deeply, intensely, with all the strength of a clinging nature. In her eyes he was perfection—good, noble and handsome. It was not surprising to her that he had sought to withdraw himself from his engagement to a girl, whose lot had been overshadowed by misery and poverty almost from the moment of her birth. Happiness and pleasure, such as come to most of us, had been denied her. She had known no bright, merry schooldays, no moments when care for the future did not haunt her. Her sole com- panion had been her sister Ethel, whose beauty had attracted numerous lovers, from amongst whom she had carefully selected the most wealthy as her spouse. Side by side the sisters had struggled wearily through their girlhood; sharing their sorrows, their food, and their clothes, and all else that Fate threw in their way. Hunger and poverty had played a cruel part in the daily lives of these two girls. Ethel, it had reduced to a cunning, unscrupulous woman; Grace to a sad-hearted girl. ,Alas ! poverty makes this beautiful world of ours a bard place to live in! But if Grace had known that Dennis loved her deeply and truly, she would have laughed at her present poverty and thought it a small matter. She could have faced the intervening time of trouble, before she became his wife, as blithely as a bird. "But he cannbt love me—he cannot! " she cried in bitterness of spirit. "The last time we met he spoke kindly—but that is long ago, more than two years. Oh, I hope he will not come—yet God only knows how deeply I love him still! If he comes he will say no ' to father. Then they will quarrel—over me ! It will be awful! I almost wish I could die." he buried her face in her pillow and sobbed.

As Grace Tewser was thus suffering tortures of mental anguish, Dennis was hurriedly preparing to leave Les Avants. To say be was packing would be deviating from the truth, for his manner of taking his belongings, one by one from Eric, and thrusting them into his gladstone could not rank as packing. "It's a deuced bore your having to start off post- haste like this!" said the latter, as he offered him some collars.

"It is," answered Dennis emphatically. "Oh, chuck those in anywhere " he added, glancing away from the collars, and proceeding to try to force some shoes into a bag. "Strikes me you're nothing of a packer," soliloquised Eric. _ _

"What do you think of doing with yourself now? Staying here ? " asked Dennis, not deigning to notice his friend's last remark.

"Yes. I shall remain here—at least, for the present." "Oh! " There was a volume of inferences con- veyed in that monosyllable. "I wish I could have stayed too," Dennis added, making vigorous efforts to close his gladstone. "I say, hold hard! There's a lot more things to go in ! " exclaimed Eric. "Then you can put them in yourself! " replied Dennis magnanimously, turning to consult "Brad- shaw." _

"Oh can I? Thanks, awfully! As it's a level impossibility, I'm not going to try! " Eric laughed ; then continued, speaking in a more serious tone: "I say, old man, why don't you make a clean sweep of it, and tell me what's up, and why you're hurrying back to England? You know what a close fellow I am."

"There's a long story hanging to it, which I can't bore you with." "It isn't about a woman, is it ? " "Yes ; the whole thing centres round a woman." "Oh " ejaculated the other expressively. "Chuck her overboard—theoretically, I mean." . . . - .

"You didn't imagine I should think you meant practically? " was the grimly spoken reply. "But that kind of thing isn't always possible." "I suppose it isn't, if you're deeply implicated. Are you ? " "Yes, on the whole. But there's nothing to be gained by talking of it." For a few minutes neither made any observation; then Dennis said: _ .

"I say, Hume, don't take into your head that there's anything discreditable in the affair. The girl is food; also pretty; and that's all I'm going to say. Eric looked at his friend in a mystified manner, llond mentally criticised his haggard, pale face. After a few minutes his brows contracted.

CHAPTER X.

"Of course you have heard that my nephew left for England last night? " observed Mrs. Hughes, speaking with her customary abruptness. 'Yes,, ' answered Gwendoline, without looking up from her drawing. "He left immediately after receiving that tele- grata, and without even saying good-bye to me!" With these words Mrs. Hughes sat down by Gwendoline's side and produced her knitting, which was her usual way of announcing her inten- tion of remaining some time. "I should certainly have liked to have had a chat with him before he left, and to find out why he was rushing home in such mad haste. Of course, his uncle did not ask any questions. It is not in the nature of men to interest themselves in the affairs of others. They only listen to what they are told. Now, 1 am never satisfied until I have thoroughly probed matters to the root. It is by far the wisest course. Don't you think so?" "It appears so, certainly," replied Gwendoline absently. "Of course it is! said Mrs. Hughes in an emphatic manner. "Besides, it seems so unnatural not to take an interest in the affairs of your fellow- creatures. Though Mr. Hughes goes to the Manor nearly every week, he never noticed last year that the Squire was having his drawing-room chairs re-covered. Of course, the first time I called with William I noticed it, and asked the Squire at once if he were going to be married. Imagine our astonishment when he said he was a married man already, and had been one for five years! Ile said he would like to introduce his wife to us, and he rang the bell and introduced us to his cook! I wished then I had not made any remark about the chairs. It was very awkward for us all. Pray don't think, though, that I wish to intimate that Dennis is going to marry his cook, or to do anything like that. But his manner of leaving yesterday makes me fear that there is a woman in the matter. Don's you think it looks suspicious ? " "it is difficult to tell," answered Gwendoline evasively. _

"But you, who know him so well, must feel quite as sure as 1 do that something very unexpected and very mrstexious has happened. Ire is not the man to pack up his clothes and hurry off without saying good-bye unless there was something he wished to keep a secret." Mrs. Hughes glanced furtively at Gwendoline. Was it possible, she asked herself, that this girl knew her nephew's secret? In that case it would be diplomatic not to enlarge on her own ignorance of it.

"When do you think Mr. Treherne will arrive at Les Avants ? she asked, changing the topic of conversation, much to Gwendoline's relief. "We hope he will ba here the day after to- morrow."

"I suppose you don't happen to know what Mr. Hurne's plans arc?" "No. I have no idea whatever. "I wonder where your sister is." "She has gone for a walk towards Glion with Mr. Hughes and Mr. Hume." "Indeed!"

---- • For a few minutes there was silence. Rut Mrs. Hughes was powerless to keep silent for very long. "I am sure you will excuse my mentioning the fact," she began with ominous slowness, "but it appears to me that Eric HUM) and your sister are not acting quite discreetly. You see, flirtations are vary serious matters. They so often lead to

t engagements and to marriago3. 1C is painful To see how flippantly your g peoplA of to-day r•gard marriage. You t aye no idea how cautiously Alr. Hughes and I approached it. He was always slow of speech, out of the pulpit ; and I am not a groat talker. So it is really surprising----" She broke off abruptly, and then added "But I wonder what took dear Dennis home so suddenly. I am very fond of him. He is such a thoroughly conscientious man—so reliable in every way. But I was told a Very strange thing about him by a poor parishioner of ours, a Mrs. Hicks, whose sister lives in London.

and knows a family of the name of Tewser. Mrs. Hicks said she had heard that he was engaged to one of these Tewsers—a very pretty girl, not corn. mon or low, but very, very poor. The father ii a retired military officer—a bad, cunning man, anti just the kind of man who would force Dennis th marry his daughter." "And you think it is true that they are engaged?' "So this Mrs. Hicks told rue. But Ido rzll! believe it, and I told her not to mention it to anyi. one else. I dislike gossip. Do you not? " "Yes—intensely." "I thought so. I should have placed more reliance on what Mrs. Hicks said if she, herself, 1214 been a reliable person, but the Hickses are ot reliable people. Maria Hicks was in our serYi6e for a year as cook. One day I saw her kissing the postman under the mistletoe! It placed me in s very awkward position, as Mr. Hughes, who is at times inconsistent, declined to interfere. He said that though kissing under the mistletoe is, strictly speaking, very worldly, still it is nowhere mentioned in the Bible as sinful. On those grounds he wanted me to let the matter pass. Of course, I would not do so. Oh! here he comes. Your sister and Mr. Hume are with him. Kindly never disclose to anyone what I have just told you about Dennis. It cannot be true."

Gwe-n-doli-neTr-o-Mieeii-;Ccrecy, then packed up her drawing paraphernalia and went to meet the others.

As ehe joined them Mr. Hughes was delivering a lay sermon, on the beauties of nature as seen from the mountains surrounding Les Avants. Eric Hume and Constance were apparently listening, but, truthfully speaking, their thoughts were more of each other than of snow-capped mountains and sunlit lakes.

Though the last few hours had brought crushing influences to bear on Dennis's and Gwendoline's affections, it had worked miracles in drawing Eric and Constance together. The possibility of a speedy separation was before their eyes, causing them to realise how hard it would be to part. Gwendoline scrutinised their faces intently and read their thoughts with unerrinc, exactitude. She earnestly trusted that Fate would be kinder to them than it had been to her and Dennis. With characteristic courage she had decided to shew a brave face to the world, and to appear as if no aching void existed in her own heart. Already she was throwing her entire energies into her painting, and working with that absorbing applica- tion which negatives all opportunities for brooding over troubles.

What a glorious godsend is work—all-engrossing work—when the heart is sad! With magical power it renders the darkest cloud transparent.

LADIES' LETTER.

My Dun NELLIE. - After the recent experiences in regard to the Weather it is well for us to be prepared for any- thing, and evidently many of the proprietors of millinery establishments are of the same opinion, for already they are showing flowers and other millinery trimming. A real novelty in headgear has a green satin bow and sprays of deeply coloured fuschias on a background of green straw, and its bows of fuschia-red velvet to give it the necessary upward tilt at the back. Another dainty affair is of green straw, with sprays of lilac arranged between a cleverly arranged drapery of mauve silk, softened wilh twists of green and mauve chiffon Which, at the beck, form a big chuu to rest on the hair.

A hat which I saw the other day was a decided novelty in the way of trimming. It was nothing else than a high and handsome comb of cut jet mounted on tortoiseshell. It makes a very dis- tinguished appearance, uprising against the black lace straw, which is all sewn with glittering Regains and veiled with filmy lace; while a circlet Of pink roses defines the limits of the fiat crown, and others cluster at the base of that comb, a high fan bow of lace being the final detail of this decidedly chic headgear.

. economical style of bodice is of satin (some deep hue like rose, pink or jonquil .yellow), entirely covered with lace. This suggests many a useful renovation. The bodice in cut should be quite plain and fitted, forming a relief to the omnipresent "pouch," which shows little sign of lessened popularity. Such a fitted bodice, with severely simple satin skirt and unlined lace sleeves, provides an evening dress of the "picture" variety, which is rightly considered the height of Rood taste, and which does not proclaim aloud in every fold its date.

WOMEN'S CULT.

On Tuesday last the celebrated Rhondda Glee Party had the honour of singing before the Queen at Windsor, the concert raking placs in St. George's flail. This building adjoins the private chspei, and her Majesty's fine organ. which was movated a few years back at a cost of L 2,000 is 64 placed as to be available both for the services in the chapel, and for concerts or recitals in the hall. s .

, Speaking of organs,the largest snecimen in the world, which is to be found in the Town flail at Sydney, Aus- tralia, has lately been greatly improved. The pitch, character.and position of the stops have been changed, two new stops have been added to wroduce thunder and chime effects,and the pipes of the choir and solo organs have been enclosed in swell boxes. The ins rument haa now five manuals and pedals, 33 thumb pistons, and 146 stops, including couplers, and tremulants. The longest pipe has a speaking length of 64 feet, and the blowing apparatus is worked by a gas engine of eight horse power, The beneficed clergy are complaining that the value of ,tithes has fallen by over 30 per cent& during the last few years,and a memorial signed by over 2.000 clergymen has been presented to Lord Salisbury praying for some relief in the ptesent heavy rating of their incomes. The adherents of the Established Clorch are officially put down as numbering thirteen millions, so that it is claimed that the grievance affecting so large a number is one of very great importance. In Great firitain,alone It is said there are nearly three hundred distinct religious sects, and the Salvation Army—probably the moat re- markable religious organisation of modern times, claims a million members. About many of the projects for celebrating the Queen of Holland's coronation next September, there is a piquant femininity. For instance, who hut a girl-Queen of eighteen would have thought of a costume exhibition on such an occasion P Queen Wilbelmina haa always shown an interest in the ancient dress of her country, and has often worn it. On the 31st of August next, she will, with her mother, the Queen Regent, attend a re- ligious service in the great church at The Hague, and six days later she will be crowned at the new church at Amsterdam. _ _

Misfortune never comes singly, and Lord Abe ma- . venny, whole broken dJwn by the disgrace and imp ri- Bounlent of his son. Lord William Nevill, has now to mourn the loss of his daughter Lady Alice Mo gland, whose death occurred the other day at Brighton. It is about ten years since, that Lord William Nevill married a daughter of the Murietta family,then one of the great financial houses of London. The Prince of Wales, with the whole of the fashionable world in his train was pre- sent at the marriage. The contrasts, between then and now,could not be more sad and pitiful. The Neville came over with the Converer, and are therefore one of the oldesp of the great English families. It was during the wars of the Roses that the Neville attained their great historical position, and at the present time they own large estates in at least seven or eight English counties. , We have long since become accustomed to the so- called" newer" types of womanhood, represented by the lady doctor, the lady journalist, the lady factory in- spector, and even lady barbers. • But with the lady auctioneer we are not as,yet so familiar. It can how- ever hardly be called the latest development, as a lady who has been in the queiness in the United States for nine years, has now been in practice in London , for several months past. She is usually taken for an American, hut is a native of this country, • and to judge by appearances, she Is doing remarkably well in the auctioneering business. Many women do not care to brave the rough surround- ' ings usually attending auction sales of furniture and effects at private houses. But if people would take the trouble and carefully make a previous aelection,it is often possible to pick up exceptional bargains in this way. One objection to going to these sales is,that they are,as a rule, attended by a class of brokers, who unless em- ployed by agents and paid a commission, bid against private purchasers. It is not horn-tide bidding, but intended simply to run up the price so as to show that it would be better to employ them. It was declared in the Birmingham County Court the other day, that this practice is illegal, and the warning that it is likely to bring those who follow it within the meshes of the law may have some effect in improving matters at private auction sales.

With regard to he arrangement of our hair, the slight- est ripple of a fringe only, is permissible. And there is no dressing at all at the back. It is all on the top, al- most over the forehead, and it is very simple and un- aggressive. The waving and general arrangement is the chief thing. We have heard much concerning the danger of certain hair washes of late, but from the frequent accidents re- corded, it would seem as though numbers of our sex yet remain,who have failed to take these warnings to heart. Only the other day, a woman, well known in societv,who had espied some grey hairs among her tresses, forthwith purchased some dye, which was specially recommended to her. The first application had an instantaneous and beneficent result, and for one evening the affect was all that could possibly be desired. But the next morning a different complexion was put upon the matter,when the lady's entire head of hair fell off, in the maid's haude ! . .

Recent chemical analyses of-Fia-ir dyes and cf;;;;;;t ice show an appalling lack of conscience in their ingre- dients, and if we will indulge in them we should he most cautious in our relections. Out of the many samples of hair dye examined at official laboratoriol, net one was free from lead,and of thirteen samples of face lotions.teu were found to contain corrosive sublimate. No woman should apply a lotion of any description unless she knows exactly what ingredients it contains.and has taken the advice of a specialist as to whether it is suitable for her use or not, Without doubt, however, it is better to ignore all so-called " skin rejuvenators" and cling to Nature's free gift of wateroun,and fresh air, with per- haps a slight massage every night. We are, it appears, to have the usual tight waistcoat with us again this spring, and pouched bodices are to be of a more modified kind. So, happily, there are not likely to be any more attempts at pouches contrived by merely pulling the basque of an ordinary bodice or jacket through the belt in an upward clivction—an arti- fice that was as .palpable as it was inexcusable.

Lace capes will be worn again this cominisPring and summer, and these of us possessed of some ancient treasures ef this description will be able to exhibit them and feel distinctly up to date. These capes thongh are not to be transparent. They will be lined with some- thing light of texture though possibly dark of tint. When the soap has completely melted put in the linen and boil it for half an hour. Then take it out, have reaslv a lather of soap and water, wash the linen in it, and then rinse it through two cold waters, with a vary little blue in the last. Heap in a baking dish and brown in the oven. "sIIADGE."

SOCIAL PROBLEMS.

THE UNEMPLOYED.

By JOSEPH RYDER. General Secretary of the

Land Nationalisation Society.

ARTICLE XVII.

A man willing to work and unable to find work is, per- haps, the saddest sight that fortune's inequality exhibits under the sun.

Thomse CARLYLE.

When in ordinary busy years autumn arrives and the leaves begin to fall • alter the harvest has been gathered, and the hop, fruit, and market gardens have given up their yield; when the nights draw in and the we tther breaks, then begins to gather in the city and the town the advance guard of the workless army. . loss Buries, M.P. Whether it be summer or winter, the unemployed like the poor, we have ever with us. And their presonce has come to be such a common thing that men cease to be surprised at it. It has Come to be regarded by most people as something that is inevitable and may as well be made the best of. But at times, as, for instance,dur- ing a prolonged spell of 'hard weather or in a period of depressed trade,the nurnbert of the unemployed increase to such a magnitude that they at last compel the atten- tion of the unthinking public. Perhaps they frighten them by Trafalgar-square demonstrations, harangued by fiery orators preac.hing a class war, or by processions of starving men through the streets. But hitherto these "excursions and alarms have always come to nothing. The great British public, moved partly by sympathy, but chiefly by fear of mob violence, bas appointed its Committees of Enquiry and lulled its conscience into sleep again. And, by-and-by, trade, which ever moves in cycles of actiiity and depression,temporarily revives, the hard weather breaks up,the spring returns,and some I (never all) of the erstwhile unemployed are absorbed Into the ranks of the actively-engaged industrial army. Then when the unemployed problem is no longer urgent, the British Public like lifrr. Gramm is disposed to pass the matter off witlia light-hearted" I I don't believe there ain't no sich a thing." And there are many who say at all times, " There is work for every- body,and none but the loafers are unemployed." How far this attitude is due to mere ignorance of the truth, how ftr to a selfish and cynical indifference, or how fir to a combination of both causes, it is imoolsible to say. There is the faot,and it constitutes perhaps the greatest obstacle in the way of dealing effectively with the ques- tion. It crops up again and again, in spite of the most convincing evidence of its falseness. It comes natural to that great class who glorify the struggle for existence In which they themselves have eucceeded,whose motto is character.lrA.:eatlEs dcaaYthboygfevenoedtsr declaring cheruNl aoen wii td, hthhoaebapntue tr improvement reshrdvot settlementee. wc i,glehf inttrtlesktdo.e of fethanhet thee give thei' ilndividual q question in te °,l t cur.to.n' ' of the unemployed is one of the most urgent of all social problems; second,that it is primarily traceable to"mact's inhumanity to roan ;" and, third, that it can never be satisfactorily dealt with apart from a fearless dealing with the land difficulty.

===== 0002601_18980225_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW MANY ARE THE 'UNEMPLOYED P

According to a modern economist " Unemployment " is the most illusive term which confronts the s'udent of modern industrial society. Authorities widely differ among themselves concerning its definition. And as there is no possible way of getting accurate statistics, no estimate of the numbers of the workless can be any- thing better than an approximate guess. Many oalcula- dons have been made at different times, ranging from Mr. Bier Hardie's statement that there are 1,760.000 un- employed, down to the opinion of the Rev. Wickham Tozer that in normal times the number of " capable, willing, sober, and industrious unemployed" does not ' exceed 140,000. The truth probably lies between the two extremes. Yet, even if we accept the smaller estim- ate, the evil disclosed thereby is surely of suffieient magnitude to call for strong remedial action. For we I must reckon not only the unemployed breadwinners,but r those also who are dependent on them. Therefore we discover at the very least there are 500.000 men,women and children in constant distress, even in normal times. That is, it is as if a vast city of the population of Bir- mingham were permanently suffering from the conse- quences of unemployment. The best judges hold that this is an understatement by at least one-half. Ind if the evil is so serious in ordinary times, what must it be when the inevitable recurrence of trade depression raises the percentage of the unemployed P But apart from the wholesale suffering which inevitably accompanies this terrible state of affairs, there is an enormsus waste of labour power,

===== 0002601_18980225_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
WAAL'S OF LABOUR POWER.

For it most be remembered that when a man is idle, whatever may be the cause, he has to be supported by those who are at work. An idle man is a national loss —erg, by what his work would have produced, and second, by what it has cost to keep him and his family. And here I would in Passing refer to the existence of an unemployed class who are net ordinarily counted in that category The Census Reports give them a name that is deemed more respectable. They are called the "unoccupied." In 1891 there were in England and Wales alone no fewer than 233,44.6 males and females between the ages of twenty and sixty-five who were not even nominal members of any trade or profession. As Mr. Hobson in his book on this question says," This represents a large mass of adult labour power utterly wasted for the purpose of social work, subsisted out pf the labour of others, and contributing nothing in return. A large proportion of this class are well-nourished, cap- able men, whose i3leneqs injures both themselves and the society in which they live. The casual voluntary work which some of these may undertake cannot be re- garded as a serious contribution to the aggregate of social work, being amateur in character and commonly misdirected, since from the economic nature of the case it is not amenable to social direction and control."

===== 0002601_18980225_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
_WORE-A RIGHT AND A DUTY.

The very firat thing to be done is to realise this one great truth—" That every man has a right to work,and that work is a duty as well as a right." Yet it is a fact beyond dispute that the foundation of our social sys- tem ilonfliSts in the ignoring of both the right and the duty. For there is no recognised right to work where there is no right to the land, which is essential before any work at all can be done. And there is no r: cognised duty to work where frme men are privileged to regard a gift of nature as their own, and to live in luxurious indolence npin the tribute which they thereby compel the landless to pay thorn. Doubtless there are many sub- sidiary causes which contribute to the existence of the unemployed, such as the increaqin2 use of machinery, changes of fashion.and variations of trade. And there are many among the " unoccupied " rich who are not landlords. But in the last analysis it will be found that the one primary cause fee the existence of bath classes is the law which establishes the pernicious privilege of a monopolist class at this expense of the birth- rights and necessities of the rest of the corn- munity.Thern is work for allot° long as any human wants are unsatisfied. There is land in plenty, if it were only i available. And there is labour in ahuadance, if only it I were properly utilised. _Ls it was in he time of the I Corn Laws, ro it is to-day • mqii are uneniployed be- cause the land Is denied to them. Last summer I visited an agricultural vills..ge in the east of England, sad what I 13W there strengthened my conviction on this point. For,aittine on some tree-trunks on the village green. were a dozen strong men who had not been. able to get employment although it was harvest time. They were all bitterly conscious of the nardship of their position. They were all willing and respectable. And not one of them bad even an allotment. They told MA that the working men of the village had applied to the Parish Council for 24 acres of la,nd,and that the result of their application. after prolonged difficulties.was a miserable allotment of four acres to be apportioned amongst the whole of them. This case is typical of many others. Therefore I believe that I take up a strong and unassailable pnlition when I say that the settlement of the. unemployed difficulty is impossible without a settlement at the land question on democratic principles, in order that men may emnloy themselves on the public land, even though the ordinary labour market is closed against them.

WARRINGTON BUTCHERS AND THE USE

OF THE TELEPHONE.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday last week, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, the National Telephone Company sued John Marsh, Grap • penhall, for £lO, the year's rent of a telephone. The company were represented by Mr. Magnet!, the district manager for South-West Lancashire ; Mr. Browne ap- peared for the defendant,and Mr. Percy Davies appeared for Messrs. Singleton and Stephenson, a third party in the case.

Mr. Browne, at the outset, stated that the defendant adrn.tted the debt, but contended that he was entitled to be indemnified by Messrs. Singleton and Stephenson, who had taken over the telephone from him and used it since. The defendant was formerly in business at the Farmers' Stores. He rented the shop from the Lancash;re and Cheshire Fanners' Association. Limited, and his time expired on the 26th April, 1897. On his going out Messrs. Singleton and Stephenson, who were butchers in the town, arranged with the Farmers' Asso- ciation to take the premises as from the 27th April. Mr. Marsh had a telephone erected, and paid £lO in adva rice for the year commencing with 27th June, 1896 so that from the 27th April ta the 27th June Messrs. Singleton and Stephenson had the telephone for nothing. They agreed on taking the shop to take over Ur, Marsh's book debts,and on 22nd April.Mr.Singleton came to the Stores, and Miss Marsh heard him say to a gentleman who was with him that the telephone was on there, and he was eoing to take it over and have a wire to his shop in Bridge-street. Miss Marsh communicated this statement to her father, and he was agreeable to it. From the 27th April up to the present Messrs. Singleton and Stephen- son had been using the telephone. They used the trunk line, and refused to pay the trunk charges. I letter was received by Mr. Marsh from Mr. Magna!l on the subjec", and Mr. Marsh saw Mr. Singleton, who said that he was not going to have the telephone. Evidenee was given in support of the above statement by Miss Bessie Marsh, Mr. Marsh, and Miss Dooley, the cashier for Mr. Singleton and formerly for Mr. Marsh, who spoke as to the telephone having been used by her employers. Mr. Davies submitted that the manner in which the action had been brought forward tended rather to the disadvantage of his clients, as the case could not be put properly before his Honour, inasmuch as he was unable to call Mr. Magnall. Mr. Singleton had a telephone at Bridge-street, and it would have beec nonsense for him to have another. It was his intention to have a private wire from his Bridge-street shop to the Farmer's Stores. He never heard a word about the telephone until Janu- ary, when Mr. Marsh spoke to him. Mr. Singleton gave evidence, and said it was in January this year when Mr. Marsh came to see him. Big Honour thought Mr. Marsh ought to have given the National Telephone Company notice of the expira- tion of his tenancy, or when he assigned his book debts to Mr. Singleton he might have stipulated for the pay- ment for the use of the telephone. Somebody must pay for the telephone, and the question was could Mr. Marsh call upon Messrs. Singleton and Stephenson to in- demnify him for the liability that be incurred with the Telephone Company, a liability which he might have put an end to? He assumed that because of what his daughter told him Messrs. Singleton and Stephenson in- tended to take the telephone. But there must be some legal liability under a contract expressed or implied, or a liability in equity between Mr. Marsh and Messrs. Singleton and Stephenson. He could not see that there had been any, and, therefore, judgment must be given in favour of the latter.

SHOCKING CONDITION OF A. WARRINGTON HOME. COUNCILLOR 'B. ROBERTS ON THE DUTIES OF PARENTS.

Oa Friday morning at the Warrington Police Court, before Councillor Henry Roberts (chairman), Mr. J. flepherd and Councillor Smethurst, George and Selina Forster, 27, Napier-street, were charged with having on the 7th February and other days during the past six months, neglected Harold, Minnie, George,John,Joseph, Charles, Arthur, and Annie, their children in a manner likely to cause them suffering.—The children were all in Court and created quite a crowd in the neighbourhood of the box. _ .

The -Chairman asked firs. Forster if th.v were all her children, and she replied : Yes, I have 9. lie hasn't reckoned one. _ . _ _

Inspector McAteer, of the N.S.P.C.C. prosecuted, and said the children ranged in age from 11 years to 3 weeks. On the morning of the 7th February, he went in com- pany with P.C. Lewis to defendant's house, and found the male defendant and the five youngest childrea. They were most filthy, especially the two younger ones They . were almost naked, and covered with vermin bites. Arthur was lying on the remains of the sofa dressed in dirty rags and covered to the waist with excrement. De- fendant said he had not seen his wife since morning as she was out. The front bedroom occupied by the de- fendants and the four youngest children contained an iron bedstead, with two mattresses and a tick completely rotted through with filth. On this bed lay the baby of 3 weeks in its own filth, and sucking sour milk out of a porter bottle. The back bedroom occupied by the eldest girl, Emily, 15, was indescribable for filth. The bed had no covering whatever. The male defendant said he could not account for the condition of the home, !le said he was getting 28s. a week and his daughter es. and the money was spent every week. The following morning witness saw the female defendant who said she had not sufficient money. The Inspector added that he did not think the man was as guilty as his wife as h- had done his utmost to get her to reform. The house was in the same condition on the 18th November so that she had had time to mend. .

Mrs. Forater : What can Ido with three children which cart hardly walk.

P.C. Lewis corroborated, and in the course of his evi- dence mentioned that he fund the baby "sucking an empty porter bottle which contained sour milk.". _

_ - . Sash Forster,so Wellington-street,rnother of the male defendant supported the Inspector's statement that the children were neglected though not short of food. The Chairman (Councillor Roberts) sail the Bench did not think Forster was free from blame by any meanl. Ie circumstances like these it was his duty to give his wife what help he could, as it was hers to look after the h .use. They would adjourn the case for a month and expect to see an improvement. According to the evidence before them Mrs. Forster's neglect was simply shocking. The Inspector had made no allega- tions of drunkenness againat her, and it 1.7.19 downright laziness on her part. At any rate she could keep the children considerably cleaner than she did. He (the chairman) would ask the Inspector to make tnis I c ale known to the sanitary authorities as well, and ,;!, all oases so distinctly lthy as this. If he would lot Mr. N niman k.ow he (Mr. Roberts) would take care that it was attended to. He hoped Forster would help his wife any efforts she miatht make to improve her situation.

A terrible crime was committed on Monday aireit at Rebabr in Belguirn a small farmer and hie wife being found dead in bed, with their heads horribly battered by blews with hatchets. The previous day they had sold a cow, and it is supposed that the proceeds furnished the motive of the horrible crime. At present there is no clue to the author of the deed.

CHIEF CONSTABLE AND WEST OF IRELAND LABOURERS.

On Monday morniee, at the conclueieu of the business at the Warrington Borouen Police Court, the Chief Con- stable (Mr. Luke Talbot) said he would like to make a little reference to a case tried on the Cei February. It was the ease of a man named Michael Felt who was charged with a violent and unprovoked assault. It was 'mentioned that he was a West of Ireland labourer, and in the course of tile remarks which he (the Chief Con- stable) made to the Bench on the subject, he said that the West of Ireland labourers were a very blood-thirsty lot when iu drink. That expression appeared in some ef the aewepapers minus the words " when in drink," 4.nd his reeearks epoeared to have given considerable offeace entougst their Irish fellow-townsmen. His at- tention had been cal:el to the matter, both publicly by er,rrempendence in the newspapers, and privately by friends of hie ewn,and ko,thersfore,thought a word of es- planatiee was due (rein him. The expression was used in reference to those unfortunate West of Ireland labourers who come so often before that Court on cherger if drunkenness and violence. He was too closely 'flied wit:, the people of Ireland, both by tiee of kin- ship and friendship, to i.nake any remark which weuld at All asperse thei, geueral national character. He knew- the,u too well , nd their high gealitiee to do anything of the kind. The remark was one of wonder that the abuse of drink had the bower to transform inoffensive and hard working men into savage and dangerous fel- lows. He wished it to go forth to the public that he had no intentien whatever of aspersing the West of Ireland people as a body. He had lived amongst them, and had a high admiration for them.—Alderman flerri- sou : Your remarks were generally applied ? The Chief Constable : They had no applicatiou to the West of Ireland people as a body, but to the unfortunate people who come here when in drink.—Councillor Shaw Green: It was the omission of the words " when in drink "that caused the unpleasantness.—The Chief Constable : Yes, perhaps the reporters did not catch what I said, because I spoke in a low tone to the Bench.

piTTs' S, LONDON-ROAD, MANCHESTER. THE RENOWNED OENISRAL FURNISHERS.

LATEST PRA'SS OPINION.

"Western Telegraph," of January 1, 1898 "Few houses in Manchester occupied in the same line of busiuess are better known or enjoy a larger share of patronage than that of Samuel Pitts, late of Gaythorn, now ofthondon-road, Manchester, the complete house shop, and office furnisher. It was in 1845 that the present proprietor initiated the business, operations having been first com- menced by him in Brook-street as a cabinet maker and dealer in office furniture. By his energy and ability the concern prospered, and more accommodation becoming necessary, a move was made to the late premises In Gaythorn about 26 years since. Samuel Pitts soon acquired a name for the superior excellence of the goods he supplied, the vast range of selection offered to customers, and the house advanced with rapid strides into a foremost and leading position in the trade. Just recently the Corporation purchased the Gay- , thorn premises for street improvement purposes. and Samuel Pitts had once again to find • fresh fields and pastnres new.' This time his choice tell upen the vast edifice until latterly known as Borough Buildings, situate 100 to 120, London-road, which he has now converted into what is often termed the largest and most convenient general furnishing establishment in Man- chester. The premises occupied are enormous in extent and attractive in appearance, and are prominently and com- mandingly located. They comprise a magnificent frontagit of plate-glass windows, well stocked with high-class goods, together with extens.ve showrooms nearly 300 feet Ling on the ground floor, and offices and counting house. The first, second, and third floors are occupied by a number of stock and showrooms, and extended workshop accommodation is offered by the premises on the opposite side of London-road. The establishment throueliout has been admirably arranged, and thoroughly and elegantly fitted up with every require- ment, including electiic light throughout, hydraulic pas- senger and luggage lift to all floors, for the effective display of the splendid selection of goods, which cannot be surpassed by those of any competitor, either in extent, variety, or , general superiority. In soundness of material, good work- manship, and elegance and novelty of &sign the house has no successful rivals in Manchester, while prices will always be found of the most satisfactory character. The proprietor Is thoroughly acquainted with every eligible source of supply and in addition to his own special make the establishment displays the choicest specimens, both new and secend-hand, of other well-known manufacturers. Samuel Pitts is a thoroughly practical man, and his personal supervision is bestowed upon the business in its entirety, He is able, ener- getic,and courteous in his control, and all his dealings are marked by his methods of strict fairness and honesty. In conclusion, we should strongly advise all furnishing, whether in part or throughout, to pay this establishment a visit. The same civility and courtesy is extended to all, whether buying or not, and the firm's well.known straightforward, syste- matic method ensures all orders being satisfactorily exe- cuted.
