TriE EX IiNER.

_FEBRUARY 25th, 1898.

This country is now treated Events of the Week, to a new foreign scare almost every week. The Greek and Cretan questions were prolific sources ot sensational news until they were ousted from the place of honour by the rapid development of events in China. It is now evident that England and Russia, to say nothing of Germany,are not coming to blows over the partitionment of the Celestial Empire, and, very opportunely for the sensation-mongers,a new scene of excitement has been discovered in West Africa. For a few years past there has been a spirited rivalry between British and French pioneers in the exploitation oi the various native kingdoms 'and territories of West Africa, until at length almost all the available land has been claimed, though not always occupied,by one party or the other. Some of these teiritories are claimed by both British and French and at the present time friendly negotiations are in progress in Paris Sor the settlement of these differences. But in spite of this fact telegrams reporting al- leged French aggressions are now and then re- ceived in London, and when these telegrams are read out to the House of Commons by Mr. CHAM- BERLAIN, with all the solemn emphasis he can place upon them, they seem for the moment to provide all the elements of a first-class " crisis." But the country is beginning to look upon Mr. CHAMBERLAIN as the most alarmist member of the whole G overnment. This perverted Radical is now the worst and the most dangerous Jingo in all the Tory party. That even from a Tory point of view he needs a restraining hand is shown by:a very significent incident which happened in the IHouse of Commons on Tuesday. On Monday Mr. CHAMBERLAIN had read out one of the famous telegrams received from an imperial official in , West Africa and had accompanied the message with a few warning and almost threatening words for the special benefit of the French Government aed;people. On Tuesday,however,when the expec- tant Commons asked for further views, Mr. Cunzen, who is the direct representative of Lord SALISBURY, assumed the duty of answering ques- tions, and ix doing so took all the point out. of Mr. CnabaseerAtm's little sensation. The French Government had, it appeared, given a point-blank denial to the charge that they were guilty of any such aggression as that suggested by Mr. CHAMBERLAIN. This made it clear that it any indiscreet French officers had made the forward movement that was complained of, he would be disowned by his own superiors, but as a matter of fact the French Foreign Secretary did not believe that any officer had done anything of the kind. This little Parliamentary incident has a signific- ance which will not escape the attention of the country. It shows, in the first place, that Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is displaying a disposition to em- bitter the relations between this and other ' countries, and particularly with France. As jr WILLIAM HARCOURT put it in hisspeech at Bury on Tuesday, Mr. CHAMBERLAIN "revels in the hot; water which he helps to heat." That is a very] dangerous weakness in a statesman holding the responsible position of Colonial Secretary in a British Ministry, and mistaken as most people think Lord &mammy has been in his dealings with the Greek and some other questions, his methods are infinitely preferable to the reckless pugnacity of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN. Outside Parliament the most important political event of the week was the speech delivered by Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT at Bury. As might be expected Sir WILLIAM finds a great deal of en- couragement in the results of the bye-elections. These bye-elections show a very remarkable re- action against the Toryism that was rampant in 1895. In the twenty-nine constituencies con- tested there has been an increase of 13,000 in the Liberal votes and a decrease of 2,200 in the Tory vote. This is better from the Liberal point of view than 1892, when there was a Liberal majority in the House of Commons. As Sir W/LLIAM HARCOURT points out A similar turnover of votes at a general election would have extraordinary results. It would mean the entire sweeping away of the Tory majority, and the return to power of the Liberal party. Nor does Sir WILLIAM leave hispolitical opponents in the indisturbed enjoyment of their illusions in reference to Liberal distractions and disunion. That there are now just as there always have been differences of opinion ,in the Liberal party on certain questions may be admitted without any reserve, but that the party is in a position to win seats and to increase its majorities where seats are not won is proved by the logic of events. When the guage of public opinion is applied to all the rumours of disunion in the party they are found to be neither more nor less than sheer nonsense. The Liberal Party is clearly still regarded by probably a majority of the people of this country as the most effective instrument of political pro- gress, and all the signs of the times point to its again being placed in power at the next general election.

TRZ trial of ZOLA, the famou Therial. Zola French novelist, which has attracted T a world-wide attention,has now come to an end. ZOL& has been found guilty of having libelled a French court martial and he has been sentenced to twelve months imprisonment and a fine of three thousand francs. The interest of the trial,however, is to be found in the glimpse which it affords of the extraordinary political and social condition of the French nation at the present time. As most people are aware ZorA is now suf- fering for his advocacy of the cause of Captain DREYFUS, a French officer who was cendemno some three or four years ago to degradation and penal servitude for having, it is alleged, sold French military secrets to a foreign government. DREYFUS was condemned, on secret evidence, by a court martial, and there has since then been a growing opinion both in France and out of it that he was wrongfully convicted. But all questions of the rights and wrongs Of the original trial have been lcst sight of in the pas- sions that have been aroused by the heated controversy of the last two or three years. On the one hand those who have maintained the intiGc- ,ence of DnEyFUs have been charged with assail- ing the honour of the French army, and on the othsr the popular animosity against the unfor- tunate officer has been lashed into fury by the assertions that he, being a Jew himself, had en- deavoured to betray France to her enemies. One result is that the importance of the army has been so magnified in the popular mind that fears of a• military revolution have actually been expressed' in some quarters. Had there, been a General in the French army with a commanding reputation now would have been a favourable opportunity to put himself at the head of the State after the fashion of some other military adventurers. As it is, the result of the Zola trial is considered to be a great victory for the French General Staff-- the first victory, according to one outspoken French journalist, the Staff has won during the • last fifty years. Another consequence of the strong feeling aroused is a threatening movement against the whole of the Jewish resi- dents in France. According to some writers the feeling against the Jews is so bitter that they de- clare it might easily end in a repetition of the St.. Bartholomew Massacres with the Jews instead cf the Protestants as the victims. It is probable, however, that these fears are greatly exaggerated,, and it is quite likely, now the Zola trial is over, , that popular excitement will be allayed almost as quickly as it was raised.

From the annual report of the School Attendance Committee just issued we notice that there is school accommodation for 12,907 children in the Warrington elementary schools. This is,it is stated, ample according to the rules of the Education De- partment. The accommodation is, however, not very conveniently situateu, as in the centre of the town there is room to spare, while on the outskirts of the borough the schools are Overcrowded. There have been or are to be coabiderable enlargements of the school accommodation. The new' Wesleyan infant schools, Liverpool-road are built to accom- date 220 children, the new schools of the Sacred Heart, Whitecross will accommodate 350, and the proposed enlargement of Silver-street school will provide for an additional 160. The number of chil- dren residing within the borough attending school is 12,212. The total grants earned (including draw- ing and fee grants) by the schools in the borough • was £13,932 Os 10d. This is £162 16s 2d more-than the amount earned during the previous year, and gives the sum of .£1 9. 114 for each child in average attendance.

The report of the Chief Constable which has just been issued helps one to realize how many-sided are the duties of the police force. The apprehen— sion of the criminal or reckless does not by any means exhaust the labours of the men in blue. Dur ing the past year, for instance, the police have specially but quietly watched 127 houses during the temporary absence of the residents, and no rob- bery has been reported trom any of these places, This will be an encouragement to heads of familia to notify the police when the time for the annua holiday comes round, or when they propose to leave their domiciles temporarily. unoccupied for one reason or another. Then again the police have in the course of the twelve months restored no fewer than 204 lost children to their parents, and they have similarly dealt with 145 wandering horses cattle and sheep. When on night duty policemen have found 325 shops, warehouses and private houses offering facilities to the prowling burglar by reasons of doors or windows being left unfastened, and no doubt the attention of the owner has been called to the fact in each case. Dogs to the number of =have been seized daring the year. The bulk of them were restored to their owners, but 95 were destroyed and four managed to escape from cus- tody.

The Chief Constable mentions the fact that the police buildings have again been condemned by H.M. Inspector of Constabulary. He (that is the Inspector) "stated that unless immediate steps were taken to provide suitable accensmodation he could not again report the Warrington police establish- ment in a state of efficiency." If the Inspector were to adopt this course it would mean the withdrawal of the sum paid annually by the County Council towards the cost of the borough police, which amounts to half of the £5,957 14s. 9d. which has been spent upon the force. This is a reminder to the Corporation that no time is to be lost in finally deciding upon the site of the proposed new police buildings. It would be better even to build upon the worst site than to lose a grant of between £2,009 and £3,000. The Town Council has now, it will be seen, decided at a private meeting upon the erection of the police buildings at the Old Barracks.. The subject has been a knotty one and has excited avast amount of discussion, but we are disposed to think that the selection that has now been made. Will meet with a pretty general approval.

Mr, Justice Hawkins has made some pertinent remarks in a case of man-slaughter by a footballer while playing, which was heard at Leicester Assizes this week, and few people,—even followers of the game of football,—will dissent from his utterances, In charging the grand jury the Judge said he had been provided with :a 'copy of rules !of the Foot— ball Association, but these had nothing whatever to do with the case. If the rules of ,the football pro- vided that one man was entitled to knock down another, and he did so and killed his victim, it would still be man-slaughter. In short no rule of any society could over-ride the law of the land. While prepared to admit all this a great amount of commiseration would have been felt fc the unfortunate young delinquent had 156 been punished for his unwitting killing of another. It is therefore pleasing to and that, though convicted of manslaughter, he was 0011! bound over to come up for jud„cment when called) upon. isir.Justice Hawkins took fresh opportuna' in pronouncing sentence to pcint out that "Those who were disposed to play roughly hi football, and ' commit acts of violence upon men who stood in

A large number of the accidents—happily usually only of a minor character—which occur on the football flelcl,might be avoided by a little refl2ction on the possible issue of undue violence.

At Stockton Heath there is some little effort be- ing made to persuade property owners into a com- prehension of the dangers attending the use of privy middens, and the superiority in sanitary and Other respects of the modern water closet. Sanita- tion is making great strides all over the country, but its innovations and improvements are princi- pally confined to the towns, and the wise action of tha Stockton Heath Parish Council is one which Should commend itself very strongly to similar bodies. Many of:our:country parishes are in a de- plorable state as regards sanitary and all !natter per- taining to healthy existence,but Stockton !death is far removed from such a c,)ndition. Yet there is ?loom for improvement, and this fact being evident to the body having control of the affairs of the parish, steps are being taken in the direction indi- Cated. It will, perhaps. Strengthen their bands in such action if we quote a paragraph bearing directly on the matter, from the 'British Medical Journal." This great authority says "It is astonishing how long P,0n:1,3 of our northern- towns have clung to such abominations as privy Zaiddens,and how tenacioully the property owners adhere to the sanitary ideals of fifty years ago. During a recent apneal case before the Salford Hundred Sessions,Professor Deltspine mentioned in evidence that he had found in two samples of earth Collected by the sanitary inspector from crevices of , the ashpit the bacilli of typhoid fever. Since the adoption of notification Dr. Crocker has had 325 Cases of typhoid fever reported from houses with Midden privies and only 25 ftom those with water Closets."

About half-past two on Saturday afternoon a girl attendant in the shop of Messrs. Leigh Bros., prin- ters and stationers, Buttermarket-et., Warrington, discovered a fire smouldering in the printing shop. She immediately informed Police-constable John Cooper, who with exemplary promptitude procured two buckets of water and dashed them on the burn- ing material, apparently waste paper. The fire had, however, got a hold which Nailed his efforts, and the fire brigade, who were just turning out for Practice, were soon on the scene, and promptly ex- tinguished the fire. Considerable damage was done to the printing shop, machinery, and fittings. LAMPS. —GiarAND's, Bridge-street, Warrington. THE INDEPENDENT LABOUR PARTY. -

SUDDEN DEATH AT RUNCORN.

On Thursday the police bad reported to them the sudden death of the five months old child (George) of George Bott, of Lyon-street, Runcorn. The child was put to sleep on Wednesday morning, he appearing in his usual health. Next morning it ate its food and shortly afterwards the mother sim that it was ill. Dr. McLennan was sent for but before his arrival the child had expired. BRUNSWICK MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT CLASS.

AN OLD OFFENCE.

Before Mr. Taylor and Mr. W- Timmins at the Runcorn Police Court on Wednesday morning, Henry Ridge was charged with having on the 22nd August 18915 kept a diqorderly hope in Hitchmough Court, Runcorn, and Mary Ba.nett was charged with aiding and abetting. Superintendent Okell said when the summonses were issued prisoners decamped from the town, and a warrant was taken out and sent to Garston, Salford, and Manchester, hut the police did not succeed in apprehending them. On Wednesday evening the fortnightly meeting of the abovo Band of Hope Society was held in the schcedroom, the Rev. J. Osborn) presiding. There was a large atten- dance of parents and children. OUGHT A WOMAN TO HAVE A VOTE P At the meeting ef the Runcorn Congregational Church Guild on Wednesday evening, the above was the title of a paper introduced by Mrs. Osborne, wife of the pastor, the Rev. J. Osborne. Tne essayist stated that taxation and representation ought to go together, and since there were women householders and women who possessed property in their own rigtts they ought to be repre- sented in Parliament. A discussion was led by Mr. William Handley who, although he raised one or two objections, agreed that it was only right and just that women should have the power to vote. Mrs. Baker warmly advocated the suffrage of women. Miss Clayden, Mr. Brookes Handley, Mr. Morris, Mr. E. Speakman,Mr. Hutton, and Mr. Beck (the Chairman) also spoke in favour of the essayist's views. Mr. Beck put the ques- tion to the meeting and it was carried by a large major- ity,there being only one dissentient voice.

FUNERAL OF MR. W. S. PRITCHARD, OF WIDNRS. On Friday,the remains of the late Mr. W. S. Pritchar I, 58 years manager of the Lancaehire metal works Widnes, was interred at the Farnworth Parish Church. The deceased gentleman had been a sufferer for some months from heart disease and dropsy, and despite the careful attentions of Dr. Donnelley, with Drs. Barr, Robinson, and Eatock in consultation, he expired en Wednesday morning at his residence, Greenbank, Farnworth. He has been a resident in Widnes for about SO years, and during 28 years of that time he acted as manager of the Laiicashire metal works. Previous to that be resided in Runcorn, where he received his training as an engineer, under the late Mr. Ebenezer Timmins. He was deeply respected in the district, and his death is a distinct loss. Principal amongst the mourners were Messrs. Joseph W. Pritchard (son), Joseph Pritchard (brother),Rancorn ; J. Williams, F. Wareing, Liverpool ; G. Pritchard, and the Rev. H. Roberts. There were also present the Mayor (Alderman Wareing), Major Lewis, Messrs. J. Morrison (district manager of the United Alkali C 0.,) C. Dritfield, M. L. Davies, P. Tallon, J. ST. EDWARD'S DAT SCROOLS.-11IISULT OF EXAMINATIONS. On Sunday evening at St. Edward's Catholic Church, Runcorn, the pastor, the R-v. Father Gore read the 24th annual report of the religious examinations in the dio- cese of Shrewsbury for 1897. The report stated that the Catholic population of the diocese (which includes the whole of Cheshire and a part of Staff ardshire) continued to increase steadily. In 1895 there were 45,825 Cathe- lies, in 1896, 46,568, and in 1897, 47.034. The infant baptisms for the year amounted to 1,935, 1 more than in the preceding year, and 49 more than in 1895. Multi- plying the number of infant baptisms by 25, the esti- mated population ot the diocese woald be, according to the ordinary rule of proportion, 48,375. The inspector's report on the recent religious examination at the Run- corn Catholic School is as follows :—" The teacher has again succeeded in obtaining excellent results. The children proved themselves well equipped for battle, for their answers were prompt, accurate and very intelli- gent. Their readiness—notably the children of Stan- dard V.—to answer the queries proposed to them was ample proof that they had received from the mistress a thorough knowledge of the eubjects for examination. There was a decidedly marked improvement this year in the religious instruction of Standard HI. which was a matter for congratulation. A. deservedly excellent , result. The scholars awarded prizes were, Standard 1., 1 Isabella Countens ; Standard 11, Frank Hunt ; Standard 111., Edward Stanton ; Standard IV., William Naugh- ton •, Standard V., James Hunt. Religious emblems in good order." The Bishop's remarks are appended : " The Inspector's report speaks sufficiently without further comment than to hope the school may ever merit so excellent a return."—Tbe secular report of the ex- aminations on the same Standards under the head- mistress Miss Barry reads :—" The advance made dur- ing the past year has been more than maintained. The school is now doing exceedingly well both in the elemen- tary and class subjeets."—The religious report of the in- fant school under the mistress Miss Hodgson reads "The infant school continues to maintain the high standard of excellence of last year. An excellent result. The religious emblems in good order." The Bishop's remarks appended are :—" In every way most satis- factory."

Anti- dotes are legion, but hot water, containing the proper proportion of Condy's Fluid, is admitted to be the simp- lest and best all-round remedy, in )(105t cases attacking the root of the evil,and acting as a preventive as well as a cure. THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, FEBRUARY 25 1898. WARRINGTON UNION NEW INFIRMARy.

LAYING THE FOUNDATEON STONES. Thursday may be regarded as one of the Red Letter days in the history of the Warrington Union for it was on that day that the foundation stones of the new infirmary were laid. The necessity for a larger:and improved infirmary to meet the growing requirements of the Union has been long felt,and it has been pressed home on the Guardians by the Local Government Board inspectors from time to time. During the past three years the subject has been repeatedly discussed by the Board of Guardians, and last year it was determined to issue tenders for a building which would meet the requirements of the Union for many years to come. Mr, Davenport, of Stockton Heath, succeeded in being selected as the builder, and the whole of the foundations of the buildinge( which will cost about .C24,ooo),have been laid. It may be well at this point to give a brief description of the proposed infirmary buildings. The Infirmary will stand upon 51 acres of land immediately behind the present Workhouse pre- mises. It will be reached from Lovely-lane, through Monks-street. There will be no connection between the grounds of the present Workhouse and this new Infirmary until the buildings are completed and ready For occupation. The present contracts will deal with three large blocks of buildings, standing some 70it. apart, but being connected on each floor with open air corridors 101 t. wide. The central will be the administrative block, three stories high, and the two outer blocks will be ward pavilions, each two stories high. Under the whale of the buildings there will be a basement for access to heating and water pipes and for special purposes of ventilation and the securing of dryness. The administrative block will give accomo lation on the ground floor for the medical officer, head nurse, dispensary,wait- ing room, nurses sitting and dining rooms' linen and other stores, and on the first and second floors there will be a large number of bedrooms and other apartments for the housing of the nursing stafteach floor being fitted with baths, lavatories and closets. Both from the ground and from the first floors can access beaobtained frem this administrative build- ing, by the corridors before spoken of, to the pavi- lions, the nurses remaining in the pavilions during the time they are cm duty and returning to the ad- ministrative block when they are off duty. Access can also be obtained from the administrative block to the different airing yards, where convalescents may be sitting out or exercising. 'Die public entrance will be in the centre of the administrative bloek, and it is on each side of the public entrance door a foundation stone has been laid. The other two blocks will be ward pavilions, one for males and the other for females,each accom- modating 92 bedsor a total of 184. In each block there are two warfs of 24 beds, two wards of 18 beds,two wards of four heds,and six wards of three beds each, and each set of wards has its separate arrangement for baths, lavatories, and closets. The large amount of staircase and corridor accommoda- tion and air space required makes these pavilions each about 240 feet long. There are also ward kit- chens,linen roorns,separation lobbies, and lifts. All wards will be heated by open fireplaces, or central open fire Musgrave stoves, but in every case will this heating be augmented by hot water pipes or ra- diators. The floors throughout will be fireproof of Messrs. HOWOM and Rogers' construction, and al- though the buildings will be well equipped with stone staircases, there will be also an outer stair of iron,in each case giving from each ward or room a choice of two staircases in case of fire.

When further accommolation is required it will be obtained by erecting other pavilions, which will again be reached by open air corridors, and the total accommodation of the pavilions now erecting and those to be erected in the future, as sanctioned by the Local Government Board, will be for 320 beds, with an additional 20 beds in the present In- fectious Diseases Hospita., which it is proposed at some day to convert into a Maternity Hospital, giv- ing the grand total of 340 beds.

The contract entered into with Nil% C. W. Daven- port, of Stockton Heath, is for the erection of the three blocks named, and their connecting corridors, exclusive of the hot water and heating, and exclu- sive of the water supply for tire extinguishing pur- poses, but including the boundary walls, and amounts to -£23,266. Externally, hard grey brick will be used,with a sparing allowance of terracotta and red pressed brick dressings. Internally, the walls are covered either with her 1 plaster of a par- ticular flaish,or tiles. The character or style of the work, owing to the requirements of large window areas and judicious economy, is necessarily some- what severe, but in the designing, outline has been studied,and it is judged that from this more effect will be obtained than from expensive detail, which would be useless. The works have been designed and are under the care, during their progress, of the architects, Messrs. William and Seger Owen, of Warrington.

He hoped that the completion of the hospital I 1 might be accomplished without accident. (Hear, hear.) The interesting ceremony was brought to a conclusion by the prayers being offered by the Rev, H. RUNCORN NOTES.

In a very short time the ratepayers of the town will be celled upon to select representatives to look after their interests on the several local bodies. The Cheshire, County Council elections are fixed for Saturday, March sth ; and the elections of Urban and Rural District Councillors, Guardians and Parish Councillors are fixed for Saturday, April 2nd. The present Runcorn repre- sentatives on the County Council are Mr. S. Taylor, J.P., for the North Division, and Mr. E. Imis an for the Saudi Division. Mr. P. Speakman, the former representative of Runcorn South, has a seat on the aldermanic bench, and it is understood that he will retain this. I hear that it is probable that Mr. Taylor will net seek re-election, for business reasons, and Mr. Walter Collier has been mentioned as a candidate for the vacancy. Mr. Imison will seek re election for the South Division, and up to the present there is no mention of an opponent.

Am W. H.- JULW'S vilurro RUNCORN. -

Mr. W. H. Jude, of Liverpool, visited Runcorn on Sunday,when he held two services of " Gospel Song "In the Public Hall. The great popularity of Mr. Jude as a musician is always evidenced by the interest t.i in his movements from to to town,and it is satisfarrory to find that Runeorn people gene/.tli are not stow to appreciate his abilities. In the afternoon Mr. Jude de- livered an eloquent and impressiv, addre is, ent.tled Saved frees the Storm," illustrating his remarks with " several musical gems, which included Geunod's " Peace of God " and Sir Ar'hur Sullivan's " Looking Back." , Re also sang several hymns from "Music and the Higher Life " series,the choruses being heartily joined in by the ' audince. All the evening service Mr. Jude spoke to a crowded audianc3 from the text" God is our refuge and I our strength." remarking that the highest type of a Christian life was that in waich was found the spirit of optimistic faith and cheerfulness of disposition. It was altogether foreign to the purposes and will of God that ' men should consider a pious life as unfitting them for happiness. Christianity,said Mr. Jude,was me essential corollary of happinea,and if conversion tended to stunt a man's hearty appreciation of human joys it did not and could not spring from a healthy Motive to live the higher and the better life. With a masterly power that few living musicians possess Mr. Jude sang "Jesu Lover of my Soul," "Toe Beautiful City of Rest" (the music of which is hie own), "Hark my soul,it is the Lord" and "Behold I stand at the Door," the audience listening with apt attention to what was undoubtedly a splendid musical treat. Mr. Jude, with that kindliness of nature for which he is so well known,honoured Ellesmere-street Chapel with a flying visit. Word had gone round wet he was expected, and the large congregation waited his arrival with keen interest. Punctually at 7-30,when the service in the chapel was over, the distinguished com- poser was seen to be making his way towards the organ and in a few secoads the sacred edifice was reverberat- ing with the glorious music of Guilmant'a "Marche Milataire" followed by the subdued but lofty "Air, with variations," by Mozart. The recital only lasted about a quarter of an hour, but its effect will not readily be for- gotten by tnoee whose privii.ge it was to listn to it. Mr. Jude,during his visit to Runcorn, was the honoured guest of Mrs. Isaac Stubbs, Lynwood amigo, Norman- road, to whom he expressed the pleasure ha bad derived from his short sojourn in the town. He returned to Liverpool on M ounay afternoon.

Tea was given in the rooms to which the Sunday school teachers, choir and members of the Men's Bible Class were invited. After tea Mr. W. Shaw,naissionary,presided,and spoke in high terms of the valuable assistance rendered to the Sea- men's Missionary cause by the newly married couple. It was very nice, he said, for workers to meet on an occasion like that, and be hoped the good feeling which ' existed amongst the teachers would long continue.He re- ferred to the sale of work which is to be held on the Nth and 21st of April. He said that would be another oppor- tunity for them to show their assistance to the goad cause. Mrs. Mack,senior teacher of the school,then made a presentation on behalf of her fellow workers. Sue said she was not in the habbit of making a speech, so they would not expect much from her. It was a great plea- sure for her to be present that night.on so interesting an occasion. It was the first of its kind that they had had and she hoped it would not be the last. They had all known their friends Mr. and Mrs. Shaw,, sad worked with them for a number of years, and always found tLeat willing to do their utmost for the good of the Church and Sunday School. In conclusion the said it was with the greatest pleasure that she presented to them on behalf of her fellow workers a splendid set of carvers in case and a dozen knives and forks as a token of their esteem and goodwill.. They all joined in wishing them long life, lull of happiness and prosperity. Mr. J. price spoke on behalf of the class, Mr. R. Shaw on be- half of the choir and Mr. T. Cooke oa behalf of the Sun- day school teachers. Mr. Shaw responded on his own and also on his wife's behalf for the sifts. The company then joined in singing " For he's a jolly good fella vi." Games were then indulged in until a late heur.

SPEECHES AT THE DINNER.

After the stones had been laid, Mr. Pilling and Mr. Houghton entertained the members and others to dinner in one of the rooms at the Workhouse. An otherwise un- attractive room had been made to look bright and cheer- ful by the use of decorative material, and the introduc- tion of lowers and plants, under the superintendence of Mrs. Ward, the energetic matron of the house. An excellent repast was served, the catering being in the capable hands of Mr. Watkins, of the Conservative Club. After dinner, a number of toasts were proposed and duly honoured. Mr. George Pilling presided, and he proposed the toasts of " The Queen," and " The Prince of Wales and the Royal Family," in suitable terms, both toasts being received with enthusiasm. Mr. A. J. /mugs then proposed the toast of "The Clergy of all denominations," coupling with it the names of the Rev. T. R. Matthews and the Rev. J. H. Riddette- He said be did not hnow why elf Chairman should select him to propose that toast as he was not aware that he had any special clerical appearance. (Laughter.) They had on their Board representatives of the Established Church, and of at least one of the Nonconformist bodies. Personally, he had always felt that the points on which the religious bodies were agreed were far more essential than those on which they differed. (Hear, hear.) The points of difference were very trivial, but they were agreed on the great fundamental facts of religion. (Applause.) The Rev. T. R. MArrinews,respondieg to the toast,said his connection with the Warrington Board of Guardians had been to him a most pleasant experience. He felt that hi 3 life was now nearing the end hut however that might be he could say that he had enjoyed the time he had heen enabled to spend in the work of the Guardians very much indeed. He was very much obliged to Mr. Jolley for the way he had spoken of the clergy and the ministers connected with that Board of Guardians. He did not know that their different religious views had anything to do with the Board. (Hear, hear.) He had never found that they entered into the work the Guar- dians undertook. He was much obliged for the kind way in which the toast had been received. (Applause.) The Rev. J. H. RIDDETTE said his esteemed colleagies, Mr. Matthews,whom they all respected and some of them loved,referron to his youth. (Laughter.) He could net claim to be juvenile either in length of years or in as- pect. Be was very pleased to be thought worthy to re- spond to that toast. If be might make a frank confes- sion he should like to say that it had been his aim from the beginning of his service on that Board not to be looked upon as a representative of the clergy but simply as a humble working member of the Board to which it was an honour to belong.and with the members of which he had found it to be a very great pleasure to be asso- ciated. (Hear, bear.) For after all they had but one object in view, and that was the amelioration of the 1 condition of the people for whose welfare they worked. 1 In that work he had been able to co-operate acceptably with his brother Guardians, and he wished to thank all for the kind manner in which they had received him. (Applause.) The CII&IRM&S said he had now td propose the health of the Chief magistrate and Mayor of Warrington (Alderman Wright). (Applause.) It had given him (the Chairman) great pleasure to see the Mayor with them that night. Ever since he had been Mayor he bad shown more thought and mere respect for the Poor Law depart- ment than any previous Mayor of the town. Under these circumstances the gentlemen who had the arrangements in hand for that day's cerenteny could not overlook the Mayor. (Hear, hear.) The Town Clerk was also invited but pressure of business prevented him from being Ore. sent. He had great pleasure in submitting the toast of the health of the Mayor. (Applause). The toast was drunk with musical honours. Other speeches followed, and the meeting dispe reed about eight o'clock.

THE COUNTY COUNCIL ELECTION AT

WARRINGTON.

There will be no contest for County Council honours in Warrington. The Borough—which it may be stated is the largest non-county Borough in the country—returns three members to the Lanca- shire County Council. The retiring councillors were Alderman Francomb, J,P., Mr. F. Monks, J.P., and Mr. F. A. Roberts, formerly of Warrington, but now residing at Windermere. The last named gentleman found it impossible to continue his duties on the Council, and Councillor William Pier- point, J.P., chairman of the Warrington Liberal Association, was nominated in his stead. Five o'clock on Wednesday afternoa was the latest hour fixed for the receipt of nominations by the Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle), and it was then found that only three gentlemen had been nominated for the three vacant seats. These were Alderman Francomb, Mr. F. Monks, and Councillor William Pierpoint, and they are therefore elected on the Lancashire County Council unopposed. The follow- ing were the nominations, with the proposers, seconders, and assentors : Pierpoint William, hair and sizing merchant. SCETCOL.

ANNUAL MEETING OP SClloizats,

Au entertainment followed, when Miss Armstrong sang vivaciously "Tie Inter- fering Parrot." Donna (Lyndro) who presided, sail he was glad to sea so many taking an intereit in the Continuation School. THE REPORT.

Mr. T. GORDON (head teacher) presented his report which stated that the school had been opened 45 times and over 500 lessons had been given. The total number of attendances was 16,533, being an average of 91.8. Daring the season there had been a distribution of prizis and four lectures,and a lantern lecture by Mr. Robson Chorley. The titles of the four lectures and the lec. turers were :—" God's Glorious Creation," Mr. Gordon ; "Insects," Mr.L.Greening; "Physical Agents of Nature,' Mr. Wilcox ; "Fishing," air. Edison. 'there had been a great improvement in the regularity of attendance and the following boys had Hot MiStled:—P.Grant,J.Elorsfield, 11.HW, F. Holloway,G. Tunstall, J.Ratawal,ti.S.mcock, E. Dower, A. Platt, and H. Platt. In the testa for specie'. prizes H. Hill is awarded that for Section 1, and G. Sim cock that for Section 2. In mensuration 10 boys oh. tamed full marks,and three in writing and composition had obtained full marks. H. Hill obtained 150 out of a possible 160 in arithmetic. The Rev. H. V. PIGOT expressed his satisfaction with the report. It was necessary to make an evening school successful, that the head teacher should have his heart in the work, and he was glad to say that in IMr. Gordon they had such a one. He was I glad the boys had persevered in their work and hoped the words of advice from Mr. Dodds would not be lost on them in the next session. Letters of apology for non-attendance had been received from Mr. R. Chorley, Mr. Jolley, Mr. Reynolds, and Mr. Finney. COWARDLY ASSAULT ON A PIINCORN POLICEMAN.

HEAVY SENIENCE.

At the Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday, the magis. trates present being Sir Richard Brooke, Bart., Mr. F.J. Norman, and Mr.E.Speakman,Simuel and John Forster, brothers, of Runcorn, were charged on remand with as- sauteing P.C. Upton in Flaleon-road,Runcorn,on the 13th inst,whilst in the execution of his duty.—Prisoner pleaded guitty.—Mr. Fletcher, of Northwich,prosecute I on behalt of the police; and Mr. F.A. Lake,of Runcorn, appeared for the prisoners. . . . . .

'the prosecutik solicitor in his statement to the bench, said the offence to which the men had pleaded guilty was in the judgment of the police a very serious one,and they were obliged to press the case.lt appeared that on the 13th just, Sunday night, at 10-30, the atten tion of Constable Upton was drawn to a number of mon in Halton-road, two,of whom were the men in the dock. His at.ention was called by a noise of music and other cries. On getting near the place the men were playing a tneuth organ whist others were dancing on the foot• path, things which should obviously be checked, especi- ally on a Sunday. The constable ordered the men away. All except the Forsters obeyed the officer's command. The prisoners came out with bad expressions and re. used to go. When they declined to move on Upton nixed John Forster and proceeded to take him away when Samuel committed a very vielent assault on him. He struck him in the face and succeeded in getting hie brother released from the officer's grasp. The constaPe then got Samuel and atter a struggle they fell to tt..i ground. John returned and endeavoured to get Samuel free. The officer and his man again went to the ground and John took a running kick at the constable's head. The latter was kicked in the forehead. It was a veil serious injury and the doct-a• said it was a cletr cut to the bone. The wound was now healing and the doctor did not apprehend that any seriJus consequences would follow.

MAKE NO MISTAKE.

Many people mistake a false for a true economy. Good economy " take care of the pence, letting the pounds take care of themselves ;" bad economy is " a penny wise and a pound foolish." True economy is care- ful to make the" stitch in time which saves nine;" false economy risks the loss of a valuable horse for want of nail in a horse shoe, or the has of a fine ship for the want of a peranyworth of tar. In all matters of economy the test question should always be" Can I afford it ?" For instance, when suffering from any slight ailment, ask yourself : Can I afford to let this slight cough or little cough develop into asthma, chronic bronchitis, or consumption ? Can I afford to let this " out of Sorts" feeling which T suffer from occasionally to settle into a confirmed melancholy ? Can I afford to let my wife grow weaker day by day under household burdens, or my child,whn is now only a little delicate,run the risk of being obliged to succumb, entirely,or can I myself afford to run the risk of breaking down entirely, and giving up my work, all for want of a little precaution ? When you are told hat the excellent preparation known as Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, which is acknowledged to be the best tonic remedy ever discovered, would enable you to avoid all these dangers, the question should not be "Can I afford it ?" but " Can I afford to be without it ?" In most cases common sense will tell you that you CANNOT afford to be without it,that you cannot afford to run the risk you are now running, while so valuable a remedy as Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters is at hand.

, If your occupation is an unhealthy one, if you are ex- posed to the weather, or work long hours in close rooms; if your constitution is weakened, or your health under- mined ; or if you are in any way not " up to the mark," use Gsvnyna Evans' Quinine Bitters,The Vegetable Tonic. It never tails to strengthen the weakened system, to brace the nerves and fortify the constitution. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters is purely vegetable, and certified by Analysts to be pure and harmless. It may be safely taken by delicate ladies and children in all cases of weak ness. Many people after enjoying the good things of life,are subject to much after suffering from the inability of the Stomach to p-rform its duties. A timely use of Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters would strengthen the digestive organs, and serve to remedy the evil. We have no sympathy with those people who always complain of being ill and miserable and out of sorts, w hen a course of Gwilym Evans' Quinine, Bitters would soon set them right. Sold in bottles 2s. 9d. and 4s. 61 each, by all chemists and patent medicine venaors,or direct from the proprietors: The Quinine Bitters Manufacturing Com- pany Limited, Llanelly, South Wal,s, carriage free by parcel post. BEFORE Tag MAGISTRATES.

SMART CAPTURE OF A WARRINGTON FOWL THIEF.

On Saturday William Warburton was charged at the Warrington Borough Police Court, before Mr. Geddes and Mr. R. T. Fairclough, with stealing three fowls, value 7s. 61, from Mr. Cronshaw's hencete, Padgate- lane, that morning.—The Chief Constable said the in- habitants of Padgate-lane and district had for some time past been robbed of their hens, and plain clothes officers bad been put on special duty in the district for the pur- pose of detecting the thieves. At 10 o'clock on Thursday night Mr. Cronshaw locked up his fowlhouse, and saw that all the fowls were safe. That morning about two o'clock Constables Delahey and Newey heard a noise,and on going in the Girection of the sound they heard the screeching of fowls. They were unable to find the place where the noise came from, but they went behind a house on some land between Padgate-lane and Manches- ter-road, and from there saw the prisoner running across a field. They pursued him, and he dropped down into a gutter. Newey pounced upon him and found be had three hens in a bag. Delahey chased a second man, but could not overtake him. The man in the gutter began to struggle and shouted, "Well, now for it," and at- tempted to throw Newey over his head. Lie was taken to the police station, and on the way he managed to put his hand into his pocket and throw away a skeleton key, which the officer picked up. They afterwards found that Mr. Cronshaw's heneote had been broken into. Three fowls were missing, and near the hencote were a pair of pliers. The prisoner had been up for stealing copper wire. He belonged to a very clever gang of poachers and fowl stealers.—A sentence of three months' imprison- ment was impose], and the magistrates complimented the officers on their smartness. A MOTHER SENT TO PRISON.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Monday Morning. before Mr. F. Monks and other magistrates, Harriet Hill,wife of Joseph Hill,62,Back Dallani=lane,was I charged with neglecting her children—lnspector Mc- Ateer, N.S.P.C.C., said the defendant was the mother of five children, whose ages ranged from fourteen years to I ten months. Joseph, aged twelve, had been sent to the Clio,so that he was not included in thecase. On the 1511 February he went to the defendant's house about half past eleven in the morning. There was none there ex- cept the woman Ellen Lawton,who had Just been before I the magistratee.He asked where Mrs. Hill was, but she could not tell him. He beard a child crying.and no going upstairs he found the youngest child lying on a filthy bed naked. He remained until the mother and the other chil(iren (with the exception of the eldest, who was working) came in. He examined the children, and found that they were all filthy in the extreme. In the front room upstairs there was a tick which was rotten through. The defendant said her husband,herself and the child (with the exception of the eldest girl) slept there. The girl slept downstairs. In the frent room downstairs there was the -frame of a sofa, and on it a sack filled with chaff, and it was almost rotten. There was no bed cover, and the defendant said the girl took off a portion of her clothes and used them for a covering. He returned in the evening with Sergeant Evans,and no alteration had been made in the condition of the children or the house. The woman's husband said that she was a drunken bad woman, and he could do nothing at all with her. She pledged everything in the house, even the children's clothes. He said she drank from morning to night, and he dared not give her any money. He (the inspector)went to the house last Satur- day night at ten o'clock, and the man was sitting there perfectly sober with his children. When asked where his wife was he said, "I cannot tell you anything about her." She went out during the evening, and he had not seen her since. Defendant had been under his (the inspector's) observation for the past two or three years, and he had repeatedly warned her. —Sergeant Evans, gave corroborative evidence as to the state of the children.—Soseph Hill the defendant's hus- band, said he had told Mrs. Lawton not to came into the house again, and that if she did he should turn her out.—Mr. Monks If you take my advice you will tell her you will pitch her outside the house if she comes in again.—Defendant said her husband gave her no money. He got drank hiroself.—The police gave the man Hill a good character,and denied that they had ever seen him drunk.—Mr. El. Taylor, police court missionary, however, said he had seen the man in drink I in his hou4e.—The Chief constable : Mr. Taylor has seen him at night. Perhaps Hill is one of those sly drunkards whe never let the police see him drunk.—The Bench sent the woman to gaol for three months with hard labour.

Nothing further was scored, and the hemesters won a well contested game by a dropped goal (2 points) to nil.

THE NORTHERN CRW-COUNTRY CHAMPIONSHIP.

SUCCESS OF A WIDNES HARRIER.

The most interesting event of the season to cross- country runners in the North of England is the race for the Northern Chatnpionship,and this season, tor various reasons,the fixture aroused more interest probably than any of its 15 predecessors. The venue of the race which took place on Saturday afternoon was Trafford Park, Manchester,and although its situation was inconvenient of approach, the course was eminently suitable, consist- ing of a circuit of ai miles of undulating park land, with easy natural obstacles. This circuit had to be covered four times, making a total distance of 10 miles. There was a record entry of 23 clubs. comprising 207 runners and the actual competitors WI mbered 205. runners, of Salford, who was third last ytar was installed first favourite, but Harrison (holder), of Manchester, Entwistle (runner up), Bolton, Barlow, of Manchester, end Tennant, West Cheshire, were all strongly fancied. The weather was not ot the best, a keen wind blowing, accompanied occasionally by drizzl- ing showers- The big field of runners soon tailed out, and at the quarter distance the first flight included Hosker, of Farnworth, Widnes,Harriion and Barlow and at the half distance these three were still in front, Har- rison now leading, with Hosker, Barlow, Tennant, Crook (Salford). and Marsh following in the order' named. The result was still a puzzle, but entering the last two miles Hosker and Marsh were running I together with a good load. A dina-dong race ensued between these two right to the finish. Hosker eventually staggering in 40 yards ahead of Marsh, who was also run out. The result cams as a great surprise toi everyone ; but Hosker ran a magnificent race, and fully deserved the victory. The winner's time was 59min. 375ec. Harrison (Manchester) was 3rd, Barlow (Man- chester) 4th, Crook (Salford) sth,Houriam (Airedale)&h, Hale (Liverpool Pembroke) 7th, White (Airedale) Bth, Griffiths (Manchester) 9th, and Reeves (Crewe) 10th. Club Placings : Ist, Manchester 11,30 points ; 2nd, Salford H, 149 ! 3rd Crewe Tally-ho 11, 230 ; 4th, Farn- worth (Widnes), 233 ; sth, Crewe 11, 235, 6th,B Ron Primrose H, 250 ; 7th. Leeds A.C.13,288 ; Bth, Liverpool Sefton H, 363 ; 9th, Bolton 11, 416 ; 10th. West Cheshire H, 469 ; 11th, Liverpool Pembroke El, 476 ; 12claNelson H, 501 ; and 13th Eccles H, 591. Warrington Orford H, Northwich H, Manchester Cyclist H, North Manchester H, Broughton H, Liver- pool Gymnasium H, Liverpool H and AC, Mac- clesfield H, Airedale H, and Farnworth (Bolton) H failed to finish six runners, and are therefore un- placed. Of the 205 starters, 129 completed the die- tance,the last man to finish being R J Jonas,of the Man- chester Cyclists, who was nearly 20 minutes behind the winner. The Farnworth run finished as follows :—J Hosker, 1; A Haynes, 25; G Wright, 33; W 11 Jones, 53; T Elnghes,s7; T Houghton,64; total, 233 points.

OUR LOCAL- CIItTR,CHES.

WYCLIFFE P.S,A • SOCIETY.

The Winwick-street Co-operativo Hall was again filled on Sunday afternoon when the meeting of the Wycliffe IP.S.A. Society was held there in consequence of the irenovation of the church. There would be about 800 present. Councillor Arthur Bennett presided, and said he was pleased beyond measure that the society was progressing in the way it was. Referring to the prac- tical teaching of Christ, he said they weie all apt to think that religion was a state of the mind or of the emotions, but that was altogether contrary to all true notions of religion. After all these years in which re ligion had been in the world they were only just begin- ning to realise that religion was "being" rather than be- lieving. Action was more than belief and conduct more than mere creed. It was their duty to carry Christianity into every detail of their life. How many apparently good Christians there were who tried to take undue advantage of their customers or deal unfairly with their workmen. How absolutely rotten was in- dustrial life. It was due to the mistaken idea that re- ligion was a matter of believing rather than being. Pro- ceeding, the Chairman alluded to the want of the true spirit of Christianity in the relations between masters and men,and in public life as well as in private life. He wished to point out that if they did wrong they would have to suffer for it.—Mr. King,of Manchester, was the speaker, and he drew some useful lessons to be learned from derelicts. A solo, entitled " The King of Life. my Shepherd is "was sung very effectively by Mr. W. H. Wallington. The service was also taken part in by the Rev. J. Benson,of Leigh, Mr. B. Farrar, and Mr. David Owen.

This Distribution will be under the Supervision of the Warrington Guardian, and the Public are invited to make a most Searching Investigation into the merits of these Remedies, and Report the Results through the Daily Press. Prof. T. M. Munyon, President of the Munyon Mance opathic Horne Remedy Company, of America, has submit- ted a novel proposal. Prof. Munyon claims that he has discovered a new system of curing Rheumatism and other diseases. He proposes to distribute from the Warrington Guardian Office, next Wednesday, March 21d, 10,000 vials of his Remedies, absolutely free to all sufferers. Munyon is thus applyiog in this country a system of distribution which has been attended by striking success in London and America, as:te,tifiel by many prominent newspapers. The notion is smart. Prof. Mun- yon is a smart man. The Warrington Guardian, admiring smartness, are willing ta give tacilities for the execu- tion of the idea. Prof. Munyon comes endorsed by many prominent business men in the United States and Canada, and he has brought to us introductions from newspapers of good standing in America. The following letter explains itself To the Editor of the" Warrington Guardian." • Dear Sir,—Having discovered a positive and speedy cure for Rheumatism and other diseases, I address You in the Lope that you will give publicity to my claim. lam well aware that in making this bold assertion there will arise a strong doubt in the minds of the public as to the truth of this statemeat, and that a large majority of the people will class this new discovery with the Koch lymph failure and the Brown Sequard elixir of life de- lusion. --- • .

----- It is to overcome this prejudice that I invoke the aid of the Press. I court the fullest investigation. I would suggest that the fairest and surest way to test the efficiency of these remedies would be to make a free dis- tribution of them under the supervision of the " Warrington Guardian." With your permission I will send 10,000 vials of the Rheumatism remedy to your office, Sankey-street, Warrington, there to be given out abso- lutely free to all persons efflicted with rheumatism ; that the name and address of each person who accepts the remedy be given, and at the end of one week the parties wile have taken the medicine to be interviewed, and that the results of each case, with the special permission of the parties interested, be published in the daily pa- pers. By this means there can be no deception or fraud, and the value of this remedy will stand or fall upon the testimony of those who have tried it. Similar tests have been made in all the principal cities and towns of America' Canada, London, Liverpoe l• Manchester, Suffield, Birmingham, Leeds, York, Bolton, Preston, Blackburn, It och.lale, Bury, Stockport, Hanley Derby, and Burton.on-Trent, under the most searching investigation by the leading newspapers. The reports show that these remedies cured more than 90 per cent, of those who tried them. My rheumatism remedy will cure acute or muscular rheumatism in from one to ten days. It never fails to cure sharp shooting pains in the arms, legs, sides, back, or breast, or soreness in any part of the body. It will promptly cure swollen joints, stiff backs, lumbago, sciatica, and all pains in the hips. It seldom fade to give re- lief after one or two doses, and almost invariably cures before one bottle has been taken. My dyspepsia cure will cure any case of indigestion or stomach trouble. I will demonstrate that 9) per cent, of all kidney com- plaints, incluline Bright'a disease, can be cured by my kidney cure. I know that my catarrh cure will cure catarrh of the head, throat arid stomach—no matter how chronic or long standing. I will prove by this test that coughs and all brcnchial affections can be cured by cough cure. I will show that nervous affections and diseases of the heart are controlled and cured by my nerve and heart cure. I will prove that my cold cure will break up any form of cold in a few hours. I will convince the most sceptical that my headache cure will cor- rect any form of headache in from three to tee minutes, and that it is a splendid stomach and heart tonic. My asthma cure and asthnsa herbs, taken according to directions, will relieve any case of asthma in five minutes, and cure in a few days. I claim that my remedies for children's diseases are positive cures, and should be in every horn,. I will prove to the medical faculty that my remedies are absolutely harmless; that they will cure more than 90 per cent, of all curable diseases. Thus it will be seen that I do not claim to cure all diseases with one remedy, but have a separate cure for each disease. These remedies are absolutely harmless, pleasant to take, and ED labelled that there can be no mistake. • - • •• • • • •

Believing that there is no cause so holy and inspiring, no vocation so lofty and ennobling, nothing co sure of man's gratitude, as an hmest effort to relieve suffering humanity, I beg to remain, Yours truly, JAMES M. MEINYON.

ELECTIVE AIMITOE-9..

No fresh candidates entered the field for the posi- tion of elective auditor to the Corporation, and therefore the two retiring auditJrs—Messrs. John Harrison and James Bolton—have been re-electeJ r nopposed. THE DEPUTY CORONEE AND THE POLICE BUILDINGS. _

An inquest was bald at the Cemetery Inn, on Monday afternoon, by Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy coroner, touching the death of Squire Hayes, who died suddenly in Caveney's lodging-house,Ship-yard, on Thursday. The body was identified by the de- ceased's widow, a charwoman, living at Widnes.— Dr. Smith said he had made a post-mortem, and at- tributed death to pneumonia and pleurisy produc- ing failure of a rather dilated and weak heart.—A verdict of "Death from natural causes,' was re- turned.—Mr. Jones said he was glad to see that new police buildings were going to be erected, and he hoped provision would be made for a coroner's court. ET. BARNABAS TEA PARTY AND SOIREE. This annual event in St. Barnaba3' parish took place on Monday evening: About one hundred per- sons sat down to tea. The room had been decorated for the occagon by the Misses Vidler and Lansdale. The tables were presided over by Miss Vidler, Miss Lansdale, Mrs. Bellian, Mrs. Evans, Mrs. Roscoe. Mrs. Looker, Mrs. Bracegirdle, Mrs. Hassell, Mrs. Trevitt. and Mrs. Handles. Dancing commenced at eight o'clock, and continued until the early hours of the morning, the number present being increased to about 200. Mr. Garton acted as M.C. The music was supplied by Mr. H. C. Hope, assisted by Mr. Shakeshaft and others. A DnuNirror BREWERS' DRIVER, William Hunt, of Greenall's-avenue, Wilderspool, was charged on Monday, at Northwich, before Messrs. J. Clough and G. B. Cliff, with being drunk whilst in charge of two horses and a dray in Witton- street on Saturday.—P.C. Burgess stated that be saw defendant sitting on the front of a dray in a stooping position. He had difficulty in getting him away from the horses. lie was drunk.—Defendant, who said he was not so drunk that be could not manage the horses, was fined 58. and costs-13e. altogether. GREAT SANKEY SCHOOL BOARD.

This Board met on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. T. Halton presided, and there were also present the Rev. E. S. Jackson,Mr. Joseph Wright:Mr. G. Clarke (headmaster), and Mr. W. D. Jeans, jun. (clerk)._ The following monthly report of the headmaster was presented .—January 29th, on kooks 32'7, pre- sent 302, average 277.1 and money received 16s. 2d.; February sth, on books 330, present 305, average 272 4 fees 15s. 10d.; February 12th, on books 327, present 305, average 281-8, money received 15s. 4d.; February 19th, on books 329, present 310 average 2821, fees 15s. Bd. There are several cases of measles and chicken-pox which prevent the attendance of scholars.—The Clerk said he had received several circulars from the department, amongst others one in respect to stone throwing at trains, with a re- quest that the Headmaster should be asked to talk to the children about the objectionable practice.— The Headmaster said he did so once a fortnight.— The Clerk said that another letter he had received was to the effect that the grant for drawing and manual instruction in future would come from the Education Department. The Board proceeded to discuss the vacancy webth had been created by the death of Mr. Henry Bate, the vice-chairman.—It was decided to leave the matter in the bands of the Chairman, who said be would do his best to induce Mr. Perks to accept office.—On the motion of the Vicar,seconded by the Chairman,Mr. Wright was appointed vice-chairman in Mr. Bate's place.—Couiplimentary references were made to the valuable services which Mr. Bate had rendered for many years to the parish, and on the motion of the Vicar, seconded by the Chairman (who remarked that Mr. Bate would be a much- missed man),it was decided tn pass a vote of condo- lence with the relatives of the deceased.—This con- cluded the business.

SPIRITUALISM.

The weekly mee-tirli-g;-oiTi;WlVarrington Spiritua- list Society were held on Sunday and Monday when Mrs. Cropper, of Ashton-under-Lyne, occupied the platform at the Academy-street Temperance Hall. Her addresses were attentively listened to, and in her clairvoyance on Sunday, both afternoon and evening, nearly every delineation was recognizA. On Monday evening Mrs, Cropper took up the even- ing with medical psychometry, the accuracy of the diagnoses of the ailments of different sufferers indisputably proving the truth of the ecinnce. Remedies of a simple nature were prescribed, and the President was able to announcs the particulars of a case in which the use of the remedies, pre- scribed by the same medium on her last visit had been followed by most beneficial results.

Sr. MART'S (LATCHFORD.) The first annual dance in connection with the above church was held in Parr flail on Monday evening, and was a great succ-ss,there being no less than 350 persons present. The platform was prettily decorated with plants, ttc., kindly lent by the Cor- poration, and presented a pleasing effect. Dancing commenced about 8 o'clock to the strains of Mr. Percy Randall's efficient band,the music throughout the evening being much enjoyed. The duties of M.C. were in the capable hinds of Messrs. Delaney, Smith, Eckersley, Wells,and Bibby,and the arrange- ments for the various dances were carried out in a Praiseworthy manner. Dancing was kept up with spirit until about two o'clock on Tuesday morning. The success of the gather; ng Was mainly due to the efforts of Father Stanley, who had spared no pains to ensure its success, his object being to raise funds for renovating the schools at Latchford. ODDFELLOWSHIP AT W ARlLlNG7oN—LsiTnerros

OF Dn. BRAID& A large and representative gathering of the War- rington brethren assembled in the Prince of Wales Lodge at the Pelican Hotel on Saturday evening for the purpose of initiating Dr. Brade into the Order, and the presentation of a P.P.G.M. Jewel to Bro. S. Ryan, P.P G. J., for services rendered. Prov. G.M. S. Baldry presided. The initiation ceremony was rendered by P.G.51. Claverhouse Graham (member of the Board of Directors) of London in a most im- pressive manner. In reply to the toast of" The Manchester Unity and Board of Directors" Bro. Graham referred to the proud position the Order had attained, having .a membership of close upon 800,000 members and a capital of over .£9,2so,ooo—worth per member ..£ll 143. 10d.—an amount far ahead of any of the large Friendly Societies. He pointed out that £21,000 per week was being paid for the alleviation of sick and dig. tressed members of the Unity. He also spoke on the subject of Old Age Pensions and regretted that the Grand Master of the Order (Bro. Lukey) should ' disregard the majority vote of the Bristol A.M.C. on this question. Bro. Graham then on behalf of the Prince of Wales Lodge presented Bro. S. Ryan with a P.P.G.M.'s jewel for services rendered to his lodge and the district. Prov.C.S. J. Emson moved, and P.P.G.M. James Massey seconded, a hearty vote of thanks to Bro. Graham for his kindness in onca more coming to assist the cause of Oddfellowship in the Warrington district,and spoke in support of Old Age Pensions after which Bro. Graham made a suitable reply. Songs, icc., were rendered by several of the brethren, and the meeting was brought to a close with the usual votes of thanks.

THE_ BISHOP OF CHESTER AT WARRINGTON:

On Monday evening the Bishop of Chester (Dr. Jayne) conducted a confirmation service in St. James' Church Latchford. The candidates num- bered 151 males and females—viz., 55 from St. Tames' ; 73 from Christ Church, Latchford. and 26 from St. Thomas' Stockton 1 loath. In his first ad- dress to the candidates the Bishop spoke upon "Strength as a Christian duty."—A number of ap- propriate hymns were sung, Mr. Frank Booth pre- siding at the organ.

WARRINGTON FIELD CLTI3.

There was a good attendance at the eighth meet- ing of the winter session, held on Friday evening last, when Mr. Gillanders, of High Legh,gave a lan- tern lecture on "Plant structure; the stem and the flower." He dealt mainly with the question of plant structure from a microscopist's standpoint,and threw on the screen some beautiful slides of sec- tions of plant stems of various kinds arid at various stages of growth. He also showed sections of a few typical leaves and flowars,and commenting on each slide in turn,pointed out the fundamental similari- ties underlying superfichil diff-rences, and the gradually increasing complexity of structure from liverworts and mosses to stately forest treas.

WILL OF MR. HERMAN E. FALK.

Probate has been granted in London of the will, which bears date March 4th, 1897, of l'slr Herman Eugene Falk, of Catselough, Winsford,Ch &shire, the "father" of the salt trade, with which be had been connected since 1841, and the founder, of a Salt Chamber of Commerce in 1858,w:io died on the 12,-,h January last,eged seventy-seven years.Mr.Falk left personal estate of the gross value of £16,948 118 sd, and of the net value of £4,935 12s 31. He directed hat there should be no funeral at his decease, but that his body should be cremated at the cremator- turn nearest to the place of his death, and he re- iquested that his relations would n•t wear mourning for him for more than six mouths. He left all of his property to his wife, Mrs.A.lice Falk,who is the sole executrix of his will.

He hoped they would allow him (Mr. Markham) to express, on his own account, how deeply be would feel the loss of Mr. Thurston. DRATII OF MRS. TUNSTALL.

Mrs. Elizabeth Turistall relict of the late Mr. Thomas Tunstall, at one time grocer of Ilaydock, died on Friday last after a short and sudden illness, at her residence in Crow-lane, Newton. The 4e- ceii.ed lady came to Earlestown from Haydock about 8 years ago, where she was highly esteemed and respected. She had not be 4 la in very good health for some time, and on Wednesday morning last weak she was intending to go to her son's in Earle-street. She was just leaving the house and feeling unwell she told her daughter she would rest a little. She was taken back into tho house,and be- coming unconscious was removed to bed, and died on Friday. The funeral took place Tuesday,the body being interred at St. Thomas's Church, Ash- ton.. There was a large gathering of friends and relatives, the funeral service being carried out by the Revs. T. R. Matthews, W. 11. N. Brennan (son- in-law) Rector of Killen. The deceased lady was 72 years of age.

UNFORTUNATE INCIDENT AT EA.II,LESTOWN. About half-past seven on Wednesday morning as a workman was proceeding up Wargrave-rosel in the direction of the Vulcan Foundry he was seen to suddenly fall to the ground. Mr. Ernest Goslin who was some short distance behind,ran to the prostrate man and found he was insensible and bl-eding from wounds on the chin and throat. Ile quickly summoned assistance from the neighbouring houses. As nothing was known of the man,P.C.Schofield was sent for and a milk shandry which was passing was requisitioned and the unfortunate man was lifted in and removed to Dr. Patterson's. On the way he recovered consciousness, and gave his name as John Williams, 19 Caoper-street, florwich, and stated that he was subject to fits. He arrived in Earlestown on Tuesday night and was going to start work at the Vulcan Foundry when he was seized with a fit, and falling against the curbstone received the injuries named above. CONGREGATIONAL TEL

On Saturday evening the annual congregational tea party and concert in connection with St. John's Church, Earlestown, was held in the Mission Hall. There were about 250 persons present at tea,which was of a substan- tial character, the following ladies presiding at the tables :—Mesdames Radford, Ro',inson, Heaton, Lord, Buckley,Dale,Makindlorridge,Thorne, Whitfield,Groves, Parker, Morris,Pierpoint,Taylor ; Misses Matthews, Tal- bot, and Patterson. After tea a concert was given, the Vicar (Rev. T. R. Matthews) presiding, and as a preli- minary to vocal proceedings,said when they looked back upon the years that had gone,and saw the, afflictions of others,they ought to feel glad that they were able to at- tend their annual tea party and concert that night. Re- ferring to the Sunday school, he said that the greatest difficulty they experienced was the finding of teachers. He made an earnest appeal for voluntewrs, especially ladies, to assist in educating the young. The object of the annual tea party and concert, as they all knew,was to scrape together a few pounds for what was called the curate's stipend, and Le trusted as the result of that gathering the fund would benefit largely. In conclusion, he hoped the choir, the artistes, and Mr. Radford would do their best to make the programme a success. Under the able and efficient conductorship of Mr. Radford, or- ganist, the. following programme was submitted in a most pleasing and successful manner :—Glee, flail,smil- ing morn" (choir) ; song. NOTES AD NEWS.

While the rest of the world has advanced, Turkey, where she has not retrograded, has stood still. A science sifter on the spot tells us, says "Science Siftings," that medical science has made no progress whatever. The popular belief is that disease is God's will, and that to attempt to cure disease would he to interfere with the divine judg.ment. Our friend has frequently found cases of people sick with, smallpox entirely neglected in order that the divine will should have its own way. When a baby is suffering from croup a hen's egg is obtained,and seven holes are made in the shell. The cyntents are poured into a bowl, and the perforated shell is dipped lute some water and lifted quickly over the basin, that the water will stream into the basin. This is re- peated thirty-nine times. The contents of the basin may the,' be used for washing the little sufferer. When a man is afilicted with boils he fills his pockets with nuts,starts down some road anl throws the nuts behind him with- out once looking back, even though the city shall be burning. The theory of this treatment is that whoever picks up one of the nuts will take with it one of the boils. The fewdoctors who have administered to the natives have been received as though they were gods. Whenever travellers go into the interior of Turkey they are appealed to as though they were doctors. The aver- age Turk or Armenian seems to believe that any man wearing a European hat is competent to treat all bodily ailments.

A Malta correspondent writes to the" Times " :—" A remarkable example of the admirable spirit which in- spires the naval service has been recently displayed in the case of the Barfleur battleship, Captain R. N. Cue- tance. The ves3el, which in the ordinary course would have been ordered to England this month, was instead suddenly despatched to China. Tee officers and men went away with the utmost enthusiasm, and men whose time was up for pension at once volunteered to complete the commission. Indeed, Sc strong was the spirit that it was founl impossible to get volunteers to remain be- hind in the tend •r."

People who fondle dog's will do well to los cautious. A. young lady travelling to Moscow constantly kissed and fondled a neat little dog belonglnz to another lady. The little animal seemed well pleased and acted very man- nerly, but all remarked that it was sneezing constantly. After arrival in Moscow, the young lady was firat affec- ted with a redness of the tip of the nose, which did not yield to any remedy ; then the nose began to be painful, inflamed, and began to secrete mucus. The physician who wa3 consulted diagnosed the case as influenza. But as she constantly became worse, and the ulceration in- creased, a consultation ~.14.1 held, whrn the microscope revealed a CAS9 of glanders, which had evidently been transferred from a horse to the dog.

The German Emperor possesses a singular pack of playing cards, anci,what is Inore,be al ways u iei it. The court cards represent portraits of reigning severeigns selected by his Majesty. The queen of hearts, it is flat- tering to record, is Queen Victoria, the Qneen of Italy figures ss the queen of diamonds, and the Empresses of Russia and Austria are respectively the queens of spades and clubs. The Pope, oddly enough,is the king of spades and the King of Italy his majesty of clubs ; Leopeld 11. of Belgium is the sovereign of diamonds, and, with be- coming modesty,the Emperor William himself is the king of hearts. The Empress Augusta, it will bo noticed, is not in the collection. The principal Premiers of Europe will be flattered to know that they are the knaves .in the original pack, and in the centre of each ace is the pre- sentment of some famous actress. The Emperor owns the original plates,and copies are naturally not issued to the public.

Social Washington has been stirred to its depths by an acute case of kleptomania in high circles.dt a recent fashionable reception one of the g.,ests, bearing a well known name, went to the hostess's bedroom and was seen to take from the dressing table several valuable pins, and put them into a pocket in her bodice. ThQ hostess was made aware of this,and in bidding good-bye to her guest detained her on pretext of admiring her lace, and putting her hand into the little satin pocket drew out the ornaments, reinirkinv,"You are going off with my pins.". The sulprit murmured someting inco- herent and hastily left.

In a recent papqr on the' Mechanical Features of Electric Traction," contributed by Mr. P. Dawson to tho Institution of Mechanical Engineers, some striking figures of the spread of electric traction in the United States were given. Out of a total of nearly 15,000 mtles of tramways in America in the year 1895, 12,533 miles were worked electrically. In the same year the number of miles worked by the cablerkystem, which at one time seemed to have such a promfaing future, was only 600 miles, whilst steam was only in use on 519 miles, and horse traction was in use on rather oVcr 1,200 mica. In ' the year 1898 there were only sixteen miles of electric tramway in Great Britian and the colonies, against 618 in Germany, 120 miles in Austro-Hungary, ninety in Belgium, and sixty-seven In France.

The following statement appears in the "North China Herald" just to hand :—" There are at the present moment lone young English girls in Shanghai— possibly more—the youngest only seventeen years old, who were married in London to Chinamen, some of them members of the Chinese Legation in Loudon, brought cut by mail steamers as the wives of these Chinese, and deserted here, exposed to perils which we know of, but need not particularise. One of thern,quite a young girl, was left in an empty house with nothing in the world but her nightdress, her supposed husband having stripped the house of everything. She was discovered by a kindhearted neighbour, who heard her crying and sobbing for twelve hours continuously," These girls have no friends in Shanghai, no money, and they are afraid to come home and tell their friends what has happened.
